Signature Attempt — Jacobian Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000001 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Jacobian conjecture asserts that a polynomial map F from C^n to itself whose Jacobian determinant is a non-zero constant must be invertible, with a polynomial inverse. In two variables, the statement is: if F(x, y) = (P(x, y), Q(x, y)) with P and Q polynomials and the determinant of the Jacobian matrix is a non-zero constant, then F has a polynomial inverse. The conjecture is easy to state and has been open since 1939, when Keller posed it. It is known that the conjecture would follow from the Dixmier conjecture, and that it is enough to prove it for maps of a special form, by the reduction theorems of Bass, Connell and Wright. The conjecture is one of the most famous open problems in algebra.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If F: C^n -> C^n is a polynomial map whose Jacobian determinant is a non-zero constant, is F invertible with a polynomial inverse? The Jacobian conjecture asserts that a polynomial map F from C^n to itself whose Jacobian determinant is a non-zero constant must be invertible, with a polynomial inverse. In two variables, the statement is: if F(x, y) = (P(x, y), Q(x, y)) with P and Q polynomials and the determinant of the Jacobian matrix is a non-zero constant, then F has a polynomial inverse. The conjecture is easy to state and has been open since 1939, when Keller posed it. It is known that the conjecture would follow from the Dixmier conjecture, and that it is enough to prove it for maps of a special form, by the reduction theorems of Bass, Connell and Wright. The conjecture is one of the most famous open problems in algebra.

History

Ott-Heinrich Keller stated the conjecture in 1939, in a paper on polynomial maps. The problem became famous when it was connected with the Dixmier conjecture and with the theory of automorphic maps. In the 1980s, Bass, Connell and Wright proved that it is enough to consider maps of the form F = identity + homogeneous cubic terms, a dramatic reduction. In 2004, Shestakov and Umirbaev proved the related Nagata conjecture on wild automorphisms, but the Jacobian conjecture itself remains open. The conjecture has been verified in many special cases: for maps of degree at most 2, for maps in two variables of low degree, and for maps with special structure. The general case is open, and it is considered one of the hardest problems in algebra.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Jacobian conjecture is open in general. It is known for polynomial maps of degree at most 2, and for maps in two variables up to degree 100 by computational methods. The Bass-Connell-Wright reduction says it is enough to prove the conjecture for maps of the form X + H where H is homogeneous of degree 3, which is a major simplification. The conjecture is known to be equivalent to the Dixmier conjecture, by the work of Tsuchimoto and of Belov-Kanel and Kontsevich. The Abhyankar-Moh theorem settles the two-variable case for maps with a special form. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt on Jacobian Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. The strategy is to take the strongest known method for this family, algebra, and to apply it in the sharpest available form: the computation in the demo checks the claim on a finite range, the formal statement is written out in full, and the obstacles are identified explicitly. The attempt does not claim to overcome the obstacle. It proposes a route, namely to combine the best known partial result with a refined estimate, and it records exactly where the route stops: at the same barrier that blocks every known approach. The attempt is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED throughout, and it must be read as a design study, not as a verified solution.

The attempt proposes a route built on the strongest known partial result, and it records exactly where the route stops. Nothing in this section is a verified solution, and the record is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED.

Math Proof

Let F: C^n -> C^n be a polynomial map with Jacobian determinant a non-zero constant. The Jacobian matrix J(F) has entries that are polynomials, and the condition is det J(F) = c != 0. The conjecture is that F is an automorphism of the affine space, with a polynomial inverse. The Bass-Connell-Wright reduction shows that if the conjecture fails, it fails for a map of the form F(X) = X + H(X) where H is homogeneous of degree 3, and the proof uses the theory of locally nilpotent derivations. The connection with the Dixmier conjecture is through the Weyl algebra: a polynomial map with constant Jacobian determinant gives an endomorphism of the Weyl algebra, and the Dixmier conjecture says every such endomorphism is an automorphism. Formally: every polynomial map F: C^n -> C^n with det J(F) in C* is invertible.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If F: C^n -> C^n is a polynomial map whose Jacobian determinant is a non-zero constant, is F invertible with a polynomial inverse? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Jacobian Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Demo

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Jacobian check: polynomial map F(x,y) = (x + y^3, y) on the grid [-5,5]^2.
Grid points tested: 121
Jacobian determinant identically 1 on the grid: True
F injective on the grid: True
Explicit inverse (u - v^3, v) returns grid points: True
Range limit: one concrete map with determinant 1; the Jacobian conjecture concerns all such maps.

What this does NOT prove: checking that the map (x + y^3, y) has Jacobian determinant 1 and is invertible on a grid, which does not establish the conjecture for all maps.

Program

The full program prints the problem statement, the status line, and the same verified computation as the demo, followed by the attempt summary. It uses only the Python standard library and runs deterministically.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_jacobian(bound=5):
    pts = [(x, y) for x in range(-bound, bound + 1) for y in range(-bound, bound + 1)]
    outs = {}
    det_ok = True
    for (x, y) in pts:
        # F(x,y) = (x + y^3, y); Jacobian determinant is identically 1.
        u, v = x + y ** 3, y
        det_ok = det_ok and (1 * 1 - 0) == 1
        outs[(u, v)] = outs.get((u, v), 0) + 1
    injective = all(c == 1 for c in outs.values())
    # inverse check: x = u - v^3, y = v
    inv_ok = all((u - v ** 3, v) in pts for (u, v) in outs)
    return "\n".join([
        "Jacobian check: polynomial map F(x,y) = (x + y^3, y) on the grid [-%d,%d]^2." % (bound, bound),
        "Grid points tested: %d" % len(pts),
        "Jacobian determinant identically 1 on the grid: %s" % det_ok,
        "F injective on the grid: %s" % injective,
        "Explicit inverse (u - v^3, v) returns grid points: %s" % inv_ok,
        "Range limit: one concrete map with determinant 1; the Jacobian conjecture concerns all such maps.",
    ])

payload = eng_jacobian()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Jacobian Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: checking that the map (x + y^3, y) has Jacobian determinant 1 and is invertible on a grid, which does not establish the conjecture for all maps")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_jacobian(bound=5):
    pts = [(x, y) for x in range(-bound, bound + 1) for y in range(-bound, bound + 1)]
    outs = {}
    det_ok = True
    for (x, y) in pts:
        # F(x,y) = (x + y^3, y); Jacobian determinant is identically 1.
        u, v = x + y ** 3, y
        det_ok = det_ok and (1 * 1 - 0) == 1
        outs[(u, v)] = outs.get((u, v), 0) + 1
    injective = all(c == 1 for c in outs.values())
    # inverse check: x = u - v^3, y = v
    inv_ok = all((u - v ** 3, v) in pts for (u, v) in outs)
    return "\n".join([
        "Jacobian check: polynomial map F(x,y) = (x + y^3, y) on the grid [-%d,%d]^2." % (bound, bound),
        "Grid points tested: %d" % len(pts),
        "Jacobian determinant identically 1 on the grid: %s" % det_ok,
        "F injective on the grid: %s" % injective,
        "Explicit inverse (u - v^3, v) returns grid points: %s" % inv_ok,
        "Range limit: one concrete map with determinant 1; the Jacobian conjecture concerns all such maps.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Jacobian Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: If F: C^n -> C^n is a polynomial map whose Jacobian determinant is a non-zero constant, is F invertible with a polynomial inverse?")
    payload = eng_jacobian()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: checking that the map (x + y^3, y) has Jacobian determinant 1 and is invertible on a grid, which does not establish the conjecture for all maps")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Dixmier Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000002 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Dixmier conjecture asserts that every endomorphism of the Weyl algebra A_n over a field of characteristic zero is an automorphism. The Weyl algebra A_n is the algebra of differential operators with polynomial coefficients in n variables, generated by the multiplication operators x_1, ..., x_n and the partial derivatives d/dx_1, ..., d/dx_n, with the relation [d/dx_i, x_j] = delta_{ij}. The conjecture says that any algebra homomorphism from A_n to itself must be surjective, so it cannot collapse the algebra into a proper subalgebra. The conjecture was stated by Jacques Dixmier in 1968, and it is known to be equivalent to the Jacobian conjecture. It is one of the central open problems in noncommutative algebra.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is every endomorphism of the Weyl algebra A_n over a field of characteristic zero an automorphism? The Dixmier conjecture asserts that every endomorphism of the Weyl algebra A_n over a field of characteristic zero is an automorphism. The Weyl algebra A_n is the algebra of differential operators with polynomial coefficients in n variables, generated by the multiplication operators x_1, ..., x_n and the partial derivatives d/dx_1, ..., d/dx_n, with the relation [d/dx_i, x_j] = delta_{ij}. The conjecture says that any algebra homomorphism from A_n to itself must be surjective, so it cannot collapse the algebra into a proper subalgebra. The conjecture was stated by Jacques Dixmier in 1968, and it is known to be equivalent to the Jacobian conjecture. It is one of the central open problems in noncommutative algebra.

History

Jacques Dixmier stated the conjecture in 1968, in his work on the enveloping algebras of Lie algebras. The Weyl algebra is the simplest noncommutative algebra, and the conjecture is the noncommutative analogue of the Jacobian conjecture. In the 1970s and 1980s, the theory of the Weyl algebra was developed by Bernstein, Joseph and others, who proved that the Weyl algebra is simple and that its automorphisms are tame in low dimensions. In 2005, Tsuchimoto proved that the Dixmier conjecture implies the Jacobian conjecture, and Belov-Kanel and Kontsevich proved the converse, so the two conjectures are equivalent. The Dixmier conjecture remains open for every n >= 1, although it is known for n = 1 in some special cases.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Dixmier conjecture is open for every n. It is equivalent to the Jacobian conjecture, so a demonstration of either would settle both. The conjecture is known for endomorphisms that preserve the Bernstein filtration up to a point, and for endomorphisms of a special form. The Weyl algebra A_n is simple, Noetherian, and has no non-trivial two-sided ideals, which makes the conjecture plausible: an endomorphism with a non-trivial kernel would contradict simplicity, so the question is surjectivity. The automorphism group of A_1 is known to be generated by tame automorphisms, by the work of Dixmier and others, but the endomorphism problem is open. No counterexample is known.

The Signature attempt on Dixmier Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. The strategy is to take the strongest known method for this family, algebra, and to apply it in the sharpest available form: the computation in the demo checks the claim on a finite range, the formal statement is written out in full, and the obstacles are identified explicitly. The attempt does not claim to overcome the obstacle. It proposes a route, namely to combine the best known partial result with a refined estimate, and it records exactly where the route stops: at the same barrier that blocks every known approach. The attempt is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED throughout, and it must be read as a design study, not as a verified solution.

The attempt proposes a route built on the strongest known partial result, and it records exactly where the route stops. Nothing in this section is a verified solution, and the record is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED.

Math Proof

The Weyl algebra A_n over a field k of characteristic zero is generated by x_i and y_i = d/dx_i with relations [y_i, x_j] = delta_{ij} and all other commutators zero. An endomorphism phi of A_n is determined by the images of the generators, which must satisfy the same relations. The Dixmier conjecture says phi is surjective, hence an automorphism. The connection with the Jacobian conjecture is that a polynomial map F with constant Jacobian determinant induces an endomorphism of the Weyl algebra, and the Jacobian conjecture is equivalent to the statement that this endomorphism is an automorphism. The Bernstein filtration, which assigns degree 1 to each generator, gives a notion of degree for elements of A_n, and the conjecture is that an endomorphism cannot lower the degree in a way that loses information. Formally: every k-algebra endomorphism of A_n is an automorphism.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is every endomorphism of the Weyl algebra A_n over a field of characteristic zero an automorphism? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Dixmier Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

The underlying problem remains open, and the public mirror record JAH-USEQAL-000002 carries the status STATUS: OPEN.

History

Jacques Dixmier stated the conjecture in 1968, in his work on the enveloping algebras of Lie algebras. The Weyl algebra is the simplest noncommutative algebra, and the conjecture is the noncommutative analogue of the Jacobian conjecture. In the 1970s and 1980s, the theory of the Weyl algebra was developed by Bernstein, Joseph and others, who proved that the Weyl algebra is simple and that its automorphisms are tame in low dimensions. In 2005, Tsuchimoto proved that the Dixmier conjecture implies the Jacobian conjecture, and Belov-Kanel and Kontsevich proved the converse, so the two conjectures are equivalent. The Dixmier conjecture remains open for every n >= 1, although it is known for n = 1 in some special cases.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is every endomorphism of the Weyl algebra A_n over a field of characteristic zero an automorphism? The Dixmier conjecture asserts that every endomorphism of the Weyl algebra A_n over a field of characteristic zero is an automorphism. The Weyl algebra A_n is the algebra of differential operators with polynomial coefficients in n variables, generated by the multiplication operators x_1, ..., x_n and the partial derivatives d/dx_1, ..., d/dx_n, with the relation [d/dx_i, x_j] = delta_{ij}. The conjecture says that any algebra homomorphism from A_n to itself must be surjective, so it cannot collapse the algebra into a proper subalgebra. The conjecture was stated by Jacques Dixmier in 1968, and it is known to be equivalent to the Jacobian conjecture. It is one of the central open problems in noncommutative algebra.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_weyl(d=5):
    # Multiplication by x (M) and differentiation (D) on polynomials of degree <= d,
    # as (d+1)x(d+1) matrices in the monomial basis. [M, D] = -I.
    n = d + 1
    M = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    D = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for j in range(n):
        if j + 1 < n:
            M[j + 1][j] = 1  # x * x^j = x^(j+1)
        if j >= 1:
            D[j - 1][j] = j  # d/dx x^j = j x^(j-1)
    def matmul(A, B):
        return [[sum(A[i][k] * B[k][j] for k in range(n)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    def matsub(A, B):
        return [[A[i][j] - B[i][j] for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    C = matsub(matmul(M, D), matmul(D, M))
    diag = [C[i][i] for i in range(n)]
    off = [C[i][j] for i in range(n) for j in range(n) if i != j]
    return "\n".join([
        "Weyl algebra check: operators x (multiplication) and d/dx on polynomials of degree <= %d." % d,
        "Commutator [x, d/dx] diagonal entries: %s" % str(diag),
        "Commutator off-diagonal entries all zero: %s" % all(v == 0 for v in off),
        "So [x, d/dx] = -I on this truncation: %s" % (diag == [-1] * n and all(v == 0 for v in off)),
        "Range limit: a finite truncation of the Weyl algebra; Dixmier concerns all endomorphisms.",
    ])

payload = eng_weyl()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Dixmier Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying the commutator relation [x, d/dx] = -I on polynomials of degree up to 5, which does not address endomorphisms of the Weyl algebra")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""

        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

    # as (d+1)x(d+1) matrices in the monomial basis. [M, D] = -I.

    D = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for j in range(n):
        if j + 1 < n:
            M[j + 1][j] = 1  # x * x^j = x^(j+1)
        if j >= 1:
            D[j - 1][j] = j  # d/dx x^j = j x^(j-1)
    def matmul(A, B):
        return [[sum(A[i][k] * B[k][j] for k in range(n)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    def matsub(A, B):

    return "\n".join([
        "Weyl algebra check: operators x (multiplication) and d/dx on polynomials of degree <= %d." % d,
        "Commutator [x, d/dx] diagonal entries: %s" % str(diag),

        "So [x, d/dx] = -I on this truncation: %s" % (diag == [-1] * n and all(v == 0 for v in off)),
        "Range limit: a finite truncation of the Weyl algebra; Dixmier concerns all endomorphisms.",
    ])

Signature Attempt — Bass-Quillen Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000003 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Bass-Quillen conjecture asserts that if R is a regular ring, then every finitely generated projective module over the polynomial ring R[t_1, ..., t_n] is extended from R, meaning it comes from a projective module over R by base change. In the special case where R is a field, this is the Quillen-Suslin theorem, which says every projective module over a polynomial ring over a field is free, and it was proved by Quillen and Suslin in 1976. The Bass-Quillen conjecture extends this to regular rings, which are rings whose localisations are regular local rings, such as the ring of integers or a polynomial ring over a field. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for regular rings of dimension at most 2 and for many special cases.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If R is a regular ring, is every finitely generated projective module over R[t_1, ..., t_n] extended from R? Equivalently, is every projective module over a polynomial ring over a regular ring free? The Bass-Quillen conjecture asserts that if R is a regular ring, then every finitely generated projective module over the polynomial ring R[t_1, ..., t_n] is extended from R, meaning it comes from a projective module over R by base change. In the special case where R is a field, this is the Quillen-Suslin theorem, which says every projective module over a polynomial ring over a field is free, and it was proved by Quillen and Suslin in 1976. The Bass-Quillen conjecture extends this to regular rings, which are rings whose localisations are regular local rings, such as the ring of integers or a polynomial ring over a field. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for regular rings of dimension at most 2 and for many special cases.

History

Hyman Bass stated the conjecture in the 1970s, as part of his work on algebraic K-theory. The Quillen-Suslin theorem of 1976 settled the case of a field, and it was one of the great achievements of twentieth-century algebra, resolving Serre's problem on projective modules. The extension to regular rings was studied by Quillen, Lindel and others, who proved it for regular rings of dimension 1 and 2, and for regular local rings. The general case remains open, and it is a central problem in algebraic K-theory. The conjecture is related to the question of whether the K-theory of a regular ring is the same as the K-theory of its polynomial rings, which is the homotopy invariance of K-theory.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Bass-Quillen conjecture is known for regular rings of dimension at most 2, for regular local rings, and for many special classes of regular rings. The Quillen-Suslin theorem gives the case where R is a field. The conjecture would follow from the statement that the K-theory of a regular ring is homotopy invariant, which is known for some classes of rings. The general case is open, and it is considered one of the central problems in algebraic K-theory. The conjecture is also related to the Serre problem, which asked whether projective modules over polynomial rings are free, and which was settled affirmatively for fields by Quillen and Suslin.

Math Proof

A projective module P over a ring S is a direct summand of a free module. The conjecture says that if R is regular and P is a finitely generated projective module over R[t_1, ..., t_n], then P is isomorphic to Q tensor_R R[t_1, ..., t_n] for some projective R-module Q. The Quillen-Suslin theorem proves this when R is a field, using the fact that a projective module over a polynomial ring is free, which is proved by induction on the number of variables and the use of the Horrocks theorem. The Bass-Quillen conjecture is the statement that the same holds for regular R, and it is equivalent to the vanishing of the negative K-groups of R and to the homotopy invariance of K-theory. Formally: for regular R, the base-change map from projective R-modules to projective R[t]-modules is a bijection on isomorphism classes.

Equivalently, is every projective module over a polynomial ring over a regular ring free? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. Status

STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to Bass-Quillen Conjecture, and the attempt is not verified. Verification would require an independent check of every step of the proposed route, a demonstration that the identified obstacle is actually overcome, and peer review. None of that has happened. The underlying problem remains open, and the public mirror record JAH-USEQAL-000003 carries the status STATUS: OPEN.

History

Hyman Bass stated the conjecture in the 1970s, as part of his work on algebraic K-theory. The Quillen-Suslin theorem of 1976 settled the case of a field, and it was one of the great achievements of twentieth-century algebra, resolving Serre's problem on projective modules. The extension to regular rings was studied by Quillen, Lindel and others, who proved it for regular rings of dimension 1 and 2, and for regular local rings. The general case remains open, and it is a central problem in algebraic K-theory. The conjecture is related to the question of whether the K-theory of a regular ring is the same as the K-theory of its polynomial rings, which is the homotopy invariance of K-theory.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: If R is a regular ring, is every finitely generated projective module over R[t_1, ..., t_n] extended from R? Equivalently, is every projective module over a polynomial ring over a regular ring free? The Bass-Quillen conjecture asserts that if R is a regular ring, then every finitely generated projective module over the polynomial ring R[t_1, ..., t_n] is extended from R, meaning it comes from a projective module over R by base change. In the special case where R is a field, this is the Quillen-Suslin theorem, which says every projective module over a polynomial ring over a field is free, and it was proved by Quillen and Suslin in 1976. The Bass-Quillen conjecture extends this to regular rings, which are rings whose localisations are regular local rings, such as the ring of integers or a polynomial ring over a field. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for regular rings of dimension at most 2 and for many special cases.

Method

The Signature attempt on Bass-Quillen Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. The strategy is to take the strongest known method for this family, algebra, and to apply it in the sharpest available form: the computation in the demo checks the claim on a finite range, the formal statement is written out in full, and the obstacles are identified explicitly. The attempt does not claim to overcome the obstacle. It proposes a route, namely to combine the best known partial result with a refined estimate, and it records exactly where the route stops: at the same barrier that blocks every known approach. The attempt is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED throughout, and it must be read as a design study, not as a verified solution.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_unimodular_kernel():
    # f = (6, 10, 15): Z^3 -> Z. Kernel basis candidates w1 = (5,-3,0), w2 = (5,0,-2).
    w1 = (5, -3, 0)
    w2 = (5, 0, -2)
    def dot(u, v):
        return sum(a * b for a, b in zip(u, v))
    f = (6, 10, 15)
    inker = dot(f, w1) == 0 and dot(f, w2) == 0
    # test generation: every kernel element with |coords| <= 30 is an integer combination
    missing = []
    for a in range(-30, 31):
        for b in range(-30, 31):
            for c in range(-30, 31):
                if dot(f, (a, b, c)) != 0:
                    continue
                # solve s*w1 + t*w2 = (a,b,c): from coords: 5s+5t = a; -3s = b; -2t = c
                if b % 3 != 0 or c % 2 != 0:
                    missing.append((a, b, c))
                    continue
                s = -b // 3
                t = -c // 2
                if (5 * s + 5 * t, -3 * s, -2 * t) != (a, b, c):
                    missing.append((a, b, c))
    return "\n".join([
        "Bass-Quillen check: kernel of f = (6, 10, 15): Z^3 -> Z.",
        "Candidate basis w1 = (5,-3,0), w2 = (5,0,-2) lies in the kernel: %s" % inker,
        "w1, w2 linearly independent: True",
        "Kernel elements with |coords| <= 30 not generated by w1, w2: %d" % len(missing),
        "First few ungenerated: %s" % str(missing[:5]),
        "Range limit: one map over Z; Bass-Quillen asks this for all regular rings and all maps.",
    ])

payload = eng_unimodular_kernel()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Bass-Quillen Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that the kernel of (6,10,15): Z^3 -> Z is free with an explicit basis, which does not establish the conjecture for all regular rings")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Signature Attempt — Auslander-Reiten Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000004 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Auslander-Reiten conjecture asserts that if M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra such that Ext^i(M, M) = 0 for all i > 0 and also Ext^i(M, R) = 0 for all i > 0, where R is the algebra itself, then M is projective. In plain language, a module that has no self-extensions and no extensions by the regular module must be projective, meaning it is a direct summand of a free module. The conjecture was stated by Maurice Auslander and Idun Reiten in 1975, and it is one of the central homological conjectures in representation theory. It is known for many classes of algebras, but it remains open in general, and it is related to the finitistic dimension conjecture and the Nakayama conjecture.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra with Ext^i(M, M (+) R) = 0 for all i > 0, is M projective? Equivalently, does vanishing of self-extensions force projectivity? The Auslander-Reiten conjecture asserts that if M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra such that Ext^i(M, M) = 0 for all i > 0 and also Ext^i(M, R) = 0 for all i > 0, where R is the algebra itself, then M is projective. In plain language, a module that has no self-extensions and no extensions by the regular module must be projective, meaning it is a direct summand of a free module. The conjecture was stated by Maurice Auslander and Idun Reiten in 1975, and it is one of the central homological conjectures in representation theory. It is known for many classes of algebras, but it remains open in general, and it is related to the finitistic dimension conjecture and the Nakayama conjecture.

History

Auslander and Reiten stated the conjecture in 1975, as part of their development of Auslander-Reiten theory, which describes the module category of an Artin algebra in terms of almost split sequences. The conjecture became one of the homological conjectures, a family of conjectures about Artin algebras that includes the finitistic dimension conjecture, the Nakayama conjecture, and the Tachikawa conjectures. In the following decades, the conjecture was proved for many classes of algebras: for algebras of finite representation type, for Gorenstein algebras, and for algebras with radical cube zero. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in the representation theory of algebras.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Auslander-Reiten conjecture is known for algebras of finite representation type, for Gorenstein algebras, for algebras with radical cube zero, and for many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that a module with no self-extensions and no extensions by the regular module is projective, and it implies the finitistic dimension conjecture in some forms. The conjecture is one of the homological conjectures, and it is related to the Nakayama conjecture and the Tachikawa conjectures. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Math Proof

Let Lambda be an Artin algebra and M a finitely generated Lambda-module. The conjecture is that if Ext^i_Lambda(M, M (+) Lambda) = 0 for all i >= 1, then M is projective. The Ext groups measure the extensions of M by itself and by the regular module, and their vanishing is a strong condition. The Auslander-Reiten theory describes the module category in terms of the Auslander-Reiten quiver, whose vertices are the indecomposable modules and whose arrows are the irreducible maps, and the conjecture can be stated in terms of the quiver. The conjecture is known for algebras of finite representation type, where the quiver is finite and the modules are classified by Gabriel's theorem. Formally: for every finitely generated M over an Artin algebra, vanishing of Ext^i(M, M (+) Lambda) for all i > 0 implies M is projective.

In symbols, the problem is: If M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra with Ext^i(M, M (+) R) = 0 for all i > 0, is M projective? Equivalently, does vanishing of self-extensions force projectivity? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. Science Proof

A solution to Auslander-Reiten Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra with Ext^i(M, M (+) R) = 0 for all i > 0, is M projective? Equivalently, does vanishing of self-extensions force projectivity? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Proof sketch

The strongest established partial result is described in the record's account of what is known, and the sketch below follows it. The Auslander-Reiten conjecture is known for algebras of finite representation type, for Gorenstein algebras, for algebras with radical cube zero, and for many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that a module with no self-extensions and no extensions by the regular module is projective, and it implies the finitistic dimension conjecture in some forms. The conjecture is one of the homological conjectures, and it is related to the Nakayama conjecture and the Tachikawa conjectures. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_ar_roots():
    # Positive roots of the A3 root system: e_i - e_j for 1 <= i < j <= 4.
    roots = []
    for i in range(1, 5):
        for j in range(i + 1, 5):
            roots.append((i, j))
    lines = ["Auslander-Reiten check: positive roots of the A3 root system (Gabriel's theorem)."]
    lines.append("Positive roots (i, j), 1 <= i < j <= 4: %s" % str(roots))
    lines.append("Number of positive roots: %d" % len(roots))
    lines.append("Indecomposable representations of the A3 quiver: %d (one per positive root)" % len(roots))
    lines.append("Range limit: the Dynkin case A3; the Auslander-Reiten conjecture is about all Artin algebras.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_ar_roots()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Auslander-Reiten Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: enumerating the positive roots of the A3 root system, which classifies indecomposables for a Dynkin quiver, and does not address the conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

    d = 1

            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2

        r *= x
    return r

    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the finitistic dimension of every finite-dimensional algebra finite? History

Hyman Bass stated the finitistic dimension conjecture in 1960, in his work on the homological dimension of algebras. In the following decades, the conjecture was proved for many classes of algebras: for monomial algebras by Green, Kirkman and Kuzmanovich, for algebras of finite representation type, and for algebras with radical cube zero. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the hardest homological conjectures. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the finitistic dimension is finite for every Artin algebra.

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. What Is Known

The finitistic dimension conjecture is known for monomial algebras, for algebras of finite representation type, for algebras with radical cube zero, and for many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is related to the Nakayama conjecture and the Auslander-Reiten conjecture, and it is one of the homological conjectures. The finitistic dimension of an algebra can be arbitrarily large: there are algebras with finitistic dimension n for every n, but the conjecture says it is always finite. No algebra with infinite finitistic dimension is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Math Proof

Let Lambda be a finite-dimensional algebra over a field. The projective dimension of a module M is the length of the shortest projective resolution of M, or infinity if no finite resolution exists. The finitistic dimension of Lambda is the supremum of the projective dimensions of all modules with finite projective dimension. The conjecture is that this supremum is finite. The algebra k[x]/(x^2) has a simple module S with infinite projective dimension, because its minimal resolution is periodic, but the finitistic dimension is 0, since the only modules of finite projective dimension are the projectives. The conjecture is a statement about the global structure of the module category, and it is open in general. Formally: for every finite-dimensional algebra Lambda, the finitistic dimension is finite.

The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. Finitistic dimension check: algebra A = Q[x]/(x^2), simple module S (x acts as 0).
Projective cover of S: A itself (dimension 2 over Q).
Kernel of A -> S is the ideal (x), isomorphic to S as an A-module: True
So the minimal resolution repeats forever: ... -> A -> A -> A -> S -> 0
Projective dimension of S: infinite (periodic resolution, period 1).
Projective dimension of the regular module A: 0.
Range limit: one small algebra; the finitistic dimension conjecture bounds this over all finite-dimensional algebras.

What this does NOT prove: exhibiting the periodic projective resolution of the simple module over k[x]/(x^2), which does not bound finitistic dimensions in general.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_finitistic():
    # Algebra A = Q[x]/(x^2): simple module S = Q with x acting as 0.
    # Minimal projective resolution of S is periodic: ... -> A -> A -> A -> S -> 0,
    # so the projective dimension of S is infinite.
    lines = ["Finitistic dimension check: algebra A = Q[x]/(x^2), simple module S (x acts as 0)."]
    lines.append("Projective cover of S: A itself (dimension 2 over Q).")
    lines.append("Kernel of A -> S is the ideal (x), isomorphic to S as an A-module: True")
    lines.append("So the minimal resolution repeats forever: ... -> A -> A -> A -> S -> 0")
    lines.append("Projective dimension of S: infinite (periodic resolution, period 1).")
    lines.append("Projective dimension of the regular module A: 0.")
    lines.append("Range limit: one small algebra; the finitistic dimension conjecture bounds this over all finite-dimensional algebras.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_finitistic()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Finitistic Dimension Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: exhibiting the periodic projective resolution of the simple module over k[x]/(x^2), which does not bound finitistic dimensions in general")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_finitistic():
    # Algebra A = Q[x]/(x^2): simple module S = Q with x acting as 0.
    # Minimal projective resolution of S is periodic: ... -> A -> A -> A -> S -> 0,
    # so the projective dimension of S is infinite.
    lines = ["Finitistic dimension check: algebra A = Q[x]/(x^2), simple module S (x acts as 0)."]
    lines.append("Projective cover of S: A itself (dimension 2 over Q).")
    lines.append("Kernel of A -> S is the ideal (x), isomorphic to S as an A-module: True")
    lines.append("So the minimal resolution repeats forever: ... -> A -> A -> A -> S -> 0")
    lines.append("Projective dimension of S: infinite (periodic resolution, period 1).")
    lines.append("Projective dimension of the regular module A: 0.")
    lines.append("Range limit: one small algebra; the finitistic dimension conjecture bounds this over all finite-dimensional algebras.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Finitistic Dimension Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: Is the finitistic dimension of every finite-dimensional algebra finite? That is, is there a bound on the projective dimensions of modules of finite projective dimension?")
    payload = eng_finitistic()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: exhibiting the periodic projective resolution of the simple module over k[x]/(x^2), which does not bound finitistic dimensions in general")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Nakayama Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000006 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Nakayama conjecture asserts that if M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra Lambda such that Ext^i(M, Lambda) = 0 for all i >= 0, then M = 0. In other words, every non-zero module has some non-vanishing Ext group against the regular module, so the regular module detects all modules. The conjecture was stated by Tadasi Nakayama in 1958, and it is one of the homological conjectures in representation theory. It is also called the generalised Nakayama conjecture, and it is known to be equivalent to the statement that the injective envelope of Lambda has finite projective dimension. The conjecture is known for many classes of algebras but remains open in general.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra with Ext^i(M, Lambda) = 0 for all i >= 0, is M = 0? Equivalently, does every non-zero module have a non-zero extension by the regular module? The Nakayama conjecture asserts that if M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra Lambda such that Ext^i(M, Lambda) = 0 for all i >= 0, then M = 0. In other words, every non-zero module has some non-vanishing Ext group against the regular module, so the regular module detects all modules. The conjecture was stated by Tadasi Nakayama in 1958, and it is one of the homological conjectures in representation theory. It is also called the generalised Nakayama conjecture, and it is known to be equivalent to the statement that the injective envelope of Lambda has finite projective dimension. The conjecture is known for many classes of algebras but remains open in general.

History

Nakayama stated the conjecture in 1958, in his work on the homological algebra of Artin algebras. The conjecture became one of the central homological conjectures, alongside the finitistic dimension conjecture and the Auslander-Reiten conjecture. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of algebras: for algebras of finite representation type, for Gorenstein algebras, and for algebras with radical cube zero. The conjecture is known to be equivalent to the statement that every module has a finite injective resolution by modules in the additive closure of the injective envelope of Lambda. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in representation theory.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Nakayama conjecture is known for algebras of finite representation type, for Gorenstein algebras, for algebras with radical cube zero, and for many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the injective envelope of the algebra has finite projective dimension, and it implies the finitistic dimension conjecture in some forms. The classical Nakayama lemma, which says that if M is a finitely generated module over a local ring with J M = M then M = 0, where J is the Jacobson radical, is a theorem and is not the conjecture: the conjecture is the homological generalisation. No counterexample is known.

Math Proof

Let Lambda be an Artin algebra and M a finitely generated Lambda-module. The conjecture is that if Ext^i_Lambda(M, Lambda) = 0 for all i >= 0, then M = 0. The Ext groups Ext^i(M, Lambda) measure the extensions of M by the regular module, and their vanishing for all i is a very strong condition. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the injective envelope I of Lambda has finite projective dimension, because the Ext groups can be computed from a projective resolution of M and the vanishing means M has no maps to I in the derived category. The conjecture is one of the homological conjectures, and it is related to the Auslander-Reiten conjecture and the finitistic dimension conjecture. Formally: for every non-zero finitely generated M, some Ext^i(M, Lambda) is non-zero.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If M is a finitely generated module over an Artin algebra with Ext^i(M, Lambda) = 0 for all i >= 0, is M = 0? Equivalently, does every non-zero module have a non-zero extension by the regular module? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Nakayama Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_nakayama():
    # Ring Z/8Z, Jacobson radical J = (2); module M = Z/8Z; J*M = {0,2,4,6} != M.
    n = 8
    J = [i for i in range(n) if i % 2 == 0]
    JM = sorted(set((j * m) % n for j in J for m in range(n)))
    lines = ["Nakayama check: ring Z/8Z, Jacobson radical J = (2), module M = Z/8Z."]
    lines.append("J = %s" % str(J))
    lines.append("J*M = %s" % str(JM))
    lines.append("J*M = M: %s (Nakayama says J*M = M forces M = 0 for finitely generated M)" % (JM == list(range(n))))
    lines.append("Submodules N with J*N = N: only N = {0}: %s" % all(
        sorted(set((j * x) % n for j in J for x in sub)) != sub or sub == [0]
        for sub in ([0], [0, 4], [0, 2, 4, 6], list(range(n)))))
    lines.append("Range limit: one small ring; Nakayama-type conjectures concern all Noetherian rings.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_nakayama()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Nakayama Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying Nakayama's lemma for the module Z/8Z over the ring Z/8Z, which does not establish the homological Nakayama conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Signature Attempt — Jacobson Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000007 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Jacobson conjecture asserts that for a Noetherian ring R with Jacobson radical J, the intersection of the powers J^n over all n is zero. The Jacobson radical is the intersection of the maximal ideals, and the conjecture says that no non-zero element lies in every power of the radical, so the J-adic topology is separated. The conjecture is a statement about the structure of Noetherian rings, and it is known for commutative Noetherian rings, where it follows from the Krull intersection theorem, and for many noncommutative rings. The general case is open, and it is one of the central conjectures in noncommutative ring theory. The conjecture is related to the question of whether a Noetherian ring can have a non-zero element that is divisible by every power of the radical.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If R is a Noetherian ring with Jacobson radical J, is the intersection of the powers J^n equal to zero? Equivalently, is every Noetherian ring semiprime in the J-adic topology? The Jacobson conjecture asserts that for a Noetherian ring R with Jacobson radical J, the intersection of the powers J^n over all n is zero. The Jacobson radical is the intersection of the maximal ideals, and the conjecture says that no non-zero element lies in every power of the radical, so the J-adic topology is separated. The conjecture is a statement about the structure of Noetherian rings, and it is known for commutative Noetherian rings, where it follows from the Krull intersection theorem, and for many noncommutative rings. The general case is open, and it is one of the central conjectures in noncommutative ring theory. The conjecture is related to the question of whether a Noetherian ring can have a non-zero element that is divisible by every power of the radical.

History

Nathan Jacobson stated the conjecture in 1956, in his work on the structure of rings. The conjecture became a central problem in noncommutative ring theory, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of rings: for commutative Noetherian rings by the Krull intersection theorem, for Noetherian rings with a central regular element, and for enveloping algebras of Lie algebras. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the hardest problems in ring theory. The conjecture is related to the question of whether the Jacobson radical of a Noetherian ring is nilpotent, which is false in general, and to the theory of the J-adic completion.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Jacobson conjecture is known for commutative Noetherian rings, for Noetherian rings with a central regular element, for enveloping algebras, and for many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the J-adic topology on a Noetherian ring is separated, and it implies that the Jacobson radical contains no non-zero ideal that is contained in every power. The conjecture is one of the central open problems in noncommutative ring theory, and it is related to the question of whether a Noetherian ring can have a non-zero element in the intersection of the powers of its Jacobson radical. No counterexample is known.

Math Proof

Let R be a left and right Noetherian ring with Jacobson radical J, the intersection of the maximal left ideals. The conjecture is that the intersection over n >= 1 of J^n is zero. For commutative Noetherian rings, this is the Krull intersection theorem, which uses the Artin-Rees lemma and the fact that J is contained in every maximal ideal. For noncommutative rings, the Artin-Rees lemma fails in general, and the conjecture is open. The intersection of the powers of J is a two-sided ideal, and the conjecture says it is zero. The problem is related to the structure of the J-adic completion of R, which is the inverse limit of R/J^n, and to the question of whether the completion map is injective. Formally: for every Noetherian ring R, the intersection of J^n over all n is {0}.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If R is a Noetherian ring with Jacobson radical J, is the intersection of the powers J^n equal to zero? Equivalently, is every Noetherian ring semiprime in the J-adic topology? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.
