Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_jacobson_rad():
    # Z/36Z: maximal ideals (2) and (3); Jacobson radical = (6) = intersection.
    n = 36
    m2 = set(i for i in range(n) if i % 2 == 0)
    m3 = set(i for i in range(n) if i % 3 == 0)
    inter = sorted(m2 & m3)
    gen6 = sorted(set((6 * k) % n for k in range(n)))
    return "\n".join([
        "Jacobson radical check: ring Z/36Z.",
        "Maximal ideal (2) has %d elements; maximal ideal (3) has %d elements." % (len(m2), len(m3)),
        "Intersection (2) cap (3) = %s" % str(inter),
        "Ideal generated by 6 = %s" % str(gen6),
        "Jacobson radical equals (6): %s" % (inter == gen6),
        "Range limit: one small ring; the Jacobson conjecture is about all Noetherian rings.",
    ])

payload = eng_jacobson_rad()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Jacobson Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing the Jacobson radical of Z/36Z as the intersection of its maximal ideals, which does not establish the conjecture for all Noetherian rings")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Signature Attempt — Kothe Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000008 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Köthe conjecture, stated by Gottfried Köthe in 1930, asks whether the sum of two nil left ideals in any ring is a nil ideal. A left ideal is nil if every element of it is nilpotent, meaning some power of the element is zero. The sum of two nil left ideals is a left ideal, and the question is whether it is nil: is every element of the sum nilpotent? The conjecture is one of the oldest open problems in ring theory, and it is known to be true for many classes of rings, including Noetherian rings, Artinian rings, and rings with a central regular element. The general case is open, and it is equivalent to the statement that the sum of all nil left ideals is the largest nil ideal of the ring.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: In any ring, is the sum of two nil left ideals a nil ideal? Equivalently, is the sum of all nil left ideals a nil ideal? The Köthe conjecture, stated by Gottfried Köthe in 1930, asks whether the sum of two nil left ideals in any ring is a nil ideal. A left ideal is nil if every element of it is nilpotent, meaning some power of the element is zero. The sum of two nil left ideals is a left ideal, and the question is whether it is nil: is every element of the sum nilpotent? The conjecture is one of the oldest open problems in ring theory, and it is known to be true for many classes of rings, including Noetherian rings, Artinian rings, and rings with a central regular element. The general case is open, and it is equivalent to the statement that the sum of all nil left ideals is the largest nil ideal of the ring.

History

Köthe stated the conjecture in 1930, in a paper on the structure of rings. The conjecture became a central problem in ring theory, and it was studied by Jacobson, Levitzki, and others. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of rings: for Noetherian rings by Levitzki, for Artinian rings, for PI rings, and for rings with the ascending chain condition on left ideals. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the hardest problems in ring theory. The conjecture is related to the question of whether the nil radical of a ring is nil, and to the theory of the Jacobson radical. A counterexample would have to be a ring with two nil left ideals whose sum contains a non-nilpotent element, and no such ring is known.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Köthe conjecture is known for Noetherian rings, Artinian rings, PI rings, and many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the sum of all nil left ideals is a nil ideal, and to the statement that the nil radical is the largest nil ideal. The conjecture is also equivalent to the statement that a ring with no non-zero nil ideals has no non-zero nil left ideals, which is the form in which it is often stated. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt on Kothe Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. Math Proof

A left ideal L in a ring R is nil if for every x in L there is n with x^n = 0. The sum L_1 + L_2 of two nil left ideals is a left ideal, and the conjecture is that it is nil. The difficulty is that an element x + y with x in L_1 and y in L_2 need not be nilpotent, because the powers of x + y involve mixed terms that are not obviously nilpotent. For Noetherian rings, Levitzki's theorem says a nil one-sided ideal is nilpotent, so the sum is nilpotent and hence nil. The general case needs a ring that is not Noetherian, and the known counterexamples to related statements all use non-Noetherian rings. Formally: for every ring R and every pair of nil left ideals L_1, L_2, the sum L_1 + L_2 is nil.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: In any ring, is the sum of two nil left ideals a nil ideal? Equivalently, is the sum of all nil left ideals a nil ideal? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Kothe Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Demo

The demo computes a verified partial result and states what it does not establish. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports whether the digests match. Its output is:

Kothe check: nil ideals in Z/8Z.
Ideal (2) is nil: True
Ideal (4) is nil: True
Sum of ideals (2) + (4) = [0, 2, 4, 6], nil: True
Range limit: one small ring; Kothe asks whether the sum of two nil left ideals is always nil.

What this does NOT prove: verifying that the sum of the nil ideals (2) and (4) in Z/8Z is nil, which does not establish the conjecture for all rings.

Status

STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to Kothe Conjecture, and the attempt is not verified. Verification would require an independent check of every step of the proposed route, a demonstration that the identified obstacle is actually overcome, and peer review. None of that has happened. The underlying problem remains open, and the public mirror record JAH-USEQAL-000008 carries the status STATUS: OPEN.

History

Köthe stated the conjecture in 1930, in a paper on the structure of rings. The conjecture became a central problem in ring theory, and it was studied by Jacobson, Levitzki, and others. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of rings: for Noetherian rings by Levitzki, for Artinian rings, for PI rings, and for rings with the ascending chain condition on left ideals. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the hardest problems in ring theory. The conjecture is related to the question of whether the nil radical of a ring is nil, and to the theory of the Jacobson radical. A counterexample would have to be a ring with two nil left ideals whose sum contains a non-nilpotent element, and no such ring is known.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: In any ring, is the sum of two nil left ideals a nil ideal? Equivalently, is the sum of all nil left ideals a nil ideal? The Köthe conjecture, stated by Gottfried Köthe in 1930, asks whether the sum of two nil left ideals in any ring is a nil ideal. A left ideal is nil if every element of it is nilpotent, meaning some power of the element is zero. The sum of two nil left ideals is a left ideal, and the question is whether it is nil: is every element of the sum nilpotent? The conjecture is one of the oldest open problems in ring theory, and it is known to be true for many classes of rings, including Noetherian rings, Artinian rings, and rings with a central regular element. The general case is open, and it is equivalent to the statement that the sum of all nil left ideals is the largest nil ideal of the ring.

Method

The Signature attempt on Kothe Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. The strategy is to take the strongest known method for this family, algebra, and to apply it in the sharpest available form: the computation in the demo checks the claim on a finite range, the formal statement is written out in full, and the obstacles are identified explicitly. The attempt does not claim to overcome the obstacle. It proposes a route, namely to combine the best known partial result with a refined estimate, and it records exactly where the route stops: at the same barrier that blocks every known approach. The attempt is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED throughout, and it must be read as a design study, not as a verified solution.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_kothe():
    # In Z/8Z: nil ideals (2) and (4); their sum is nil.
    n = 8
    def is_nil_ideal(gen):
        ideal = sorted(set((gen * k) % n for k in range(n)))
        return all((x * x * x) % n == 0 for x in ideal)
    lines = ["Kothe check: nil ideals in Z/8Z."]
    for g in (2, 4):
        lines.append("Ideal (%d) is nil: %s" % (g, is_nil_ideal(g)))
    s = sorted(set((2 * a + 4 * b) % n for a in range(n) for b in range(n)))
    lines.append("Sum of ideals (2) + (4) = %s, nil: %s" % (str(s), all((x ** 3) % n == 0 for x in s)))
    lines.append("Range limit: one small ring; Kothe asks whether the sum of two nil left ideals is always nil.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_kothe()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Kothe Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that the sum of the nil ideals (2) and (4) in Z/8Z is nil, which does not establish the conjecture for all rings")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_kothe():

    n = 8

        return all((x * x * x) % n == 0 for x in ideal)

        lines.append("Ideal (%d) is nil: %s" % (g, is_nil_ideal(g)))

    lines.append("Sum of ideals (2) + (4) = %s, nil: %s" % (str(s), all((x ** 3) % n == 0 for x in s)))
    lines.append("Range limit: one small ring; Kothe asks whether the sum of two nil left ideals is always nil.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Kothe Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: In any ring, is the sum of two nil left ideals a nil ideal? Equivalently, is the sum of all nil left ideals a nil ideal?")
    payload = eng_kothe()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that the sum of the nil ideals (2) and (4) in Z/8Z is nil, which does not establish the conjecture for all rings")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Kaplansky Idempotent Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000009 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. Kaplansky's idempotent conjecture asserts that if K is a field and G is a torsion-free group, then the group ring K[G] has no idempotents other than 0 and 1. An idempotent is an element a with a^2 = a, and the trivial idempotents are 0 and 1. The conjecture says that the group ring of a torsion-free group is connected, in the sense that it cannot be split into two pieces by an idempotent. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for many classes of groups, including elementary amenable groups and hyperbolic groups. It is implied by the zero-divisor conjecture, which says K[G] has no zero divisors, and it is related to the Farrell-Jones conjecture, which would imply it for a large class of groups.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If K is a field and G is a torsion-free group, does the group ring K[G] contain any idempotent other than 0 and 1? Kaplansky's idempotent conjecture asserts that if K is a field and G is a torsion-free group, then the group ring K[G] has no idempotents other than 0 and 1. An idempotent is an element a with a^2 = a, and the trivial idempotents are 0 and 1. The conjecture says that the group ring of a torsion-free group is connected, in the sense that it cannot be split into two pieces by an idempotent. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for many classes of groups, including elementary amenable groups and hyperbolic groups. History

Irving Kaplansky stated the conjecture in the 1950s, as part of his work on group rings. The conjecture became one of the central problems in the theory of group rings, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of groups: for elementary amenable groups, for hyperbolic groups, and for groups with a certain geometric structure. In 2021, Giles Gardam disproved the unit conjecture, which is the strongest of Kaplansky's three conjectures, by exhibiting a non-trivial unit in a group ring, but the idempotent conjecture and the zero-divisor conjecture remain open. The idempotent conjecture is now understood as a test case for the Farrell-Jones conjecture, which would imply it.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The idempotent conjecture is open in general. It is known for elementary amenable groups, for hyperbolic groups, and for many other classes. It is implied by the zero-divisor conjecture, which is also open, and it is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture for the group in question. The unit conjecture, which is the strongest of the three, was disproved by Gardam in 2021, but the disproof does not touch the idempotent conjecture, because the unit conjecture is strictly stronger. The Kadison-Kaplansky conjecture, which is the analogue for the reduced group C*-algebra, is known for hyperbolic groups. No non-trivial idempotent in the group ring of a torsion-free group is known.

Math Proof

Let K be a field and G a torsion-free group. The group ring K[G] consists of finite formal sums sum k_g g with k_g in K, multiplied by the group law. An idempotent is an element a with a^2 = a. The conjecture is that the only idempotents are 0 and 1. The zero-divisor conjecture says K[G] is a domain, which implies the idempotent conjecture: if a is a non-trivial idempotent, then a(1 - a) = 0 gives zero divisors. The Farrell-Jones conjecture implies the idempotent conjecture because it computes the K-theory of the group ring, and the idempotents are detected by K_0. The conjecture is a statement about the algebraic structure of group rings, and it is open in general. Formally: for every field K and every torsion-free group G, the only idempotents in K[G] are 0 and 1.

The Kadison-Kaplansky conjecture, which is the analogue for the reduced group C*-algebra, is known for hyperbolic groups. No non-trivial idempotent in the group ring of a torsion-free group is known. The sketch shows the shape of the argument: a main term is identified, an error term is bounded, and the bound is too weak to reach the claim. The limits of the sketch are stated with it: the argument works on a finite range or under an unproved hypothesis, and it does not extend to the full claim. That is the honest state of the art for this problem.

Results reported

The idempotent conjecture is open in general. It is known for elementary amenable groups, for hyperbolic groups, and for many other classes. It is implied by the zero-divisor conjecture, which is also open, and it is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture for the group in question. The unit conjecture, which is the strongest of the three, was disproved by Gardam in 2021, but the disproof does not touch the idempotent conjecture, because the unit conjecture is strictly stronger. The Kadison-Kaplansky conjecture, which is the analogue for the reduced group C*-algebra, is known for hyperbolic groups. No non-trivial idempotent in the group ring of a torsion-free group is known.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_basstrace():
    # Idempotent 2x2 integer matrices E with E^2 = E; Hattori-Stallings trace = trace(E).
    def matmul(A, B):
        return [[A[0][0] * B[0][0] + A[0][1] * B[1][0], A[0][0] * B[0][1] + A[0][1] * B[1][1]],
                [A[1][0] * B[0][0] + A[1][1] * B[1][0], A[1][0] * B[0][1] + A[1][1] * B[1][1]]]
    found = []
    for a in range(-2, 3):
        for b in range(-2, 3):
            for c in range(-2, 3):
                for d in range(-2, 3):
                    E = [[a, b], [c, d]]
                    if matmul(E, E) == E:
                        found.append((E, a + d))
    lines = ["Bass trace check: 2x2 integer idempotent matrices with entries in [-2, 2]."]
    lines.append("Idempotents found: %d" % len(found))
    lines.append("Their traces: %s" % str(sorted(set(t for _, t in found))))
    lines.append("All traces are integers (0, 1 or 2): %s" % all(t in (0, 1, 2) for _, t in found))
    lines.append("Range limit: small matrices only; the Bass conjecture is about all idempotents in group rings.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_basstrace()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Kaplansky Idempotent Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: enumerating idempotents in 2x2 integer matrices and checking their traces, which does not address idempotents in group rings")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Signature Attempt — Kaplansky Direct Finiteness Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000010 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. Kaplansky's direct finiteness conjecture asserts that the group ring K[G] of any group G over any field K is directly finite: if a and b are elements of the group ring with ab = 1, then ba = 1. A ring is directly finite if a one-sided inverse is a two-sided inverse, and the conjecture says group rings always have this property. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for many classes of groups, including sofic groups, which include all amenable groups and all residually finite groups. The conjecture is related to the zero-divisor conjecture and to the theory of von Neumann algebras, where direct finiteness is a consequence of the existence of a trace. The conjecture is one of Kaplansky's conjectures on group rings, and it remains open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If K is a field and G is a group, and a, b in K[G] satisfy ab = 1, does ba = 1 follow? Equivalently, is every group ring directly finite? Kaplansky's direct finiteness conjecture asserts that the group ring K[G] of any group G over any field K is directly finite: if a and b are elements of the group ring with ab = 1, then ba = 1. A ring is directly finite if a one-sided inverse is a two-sided inverse, and the conjecture says group rings always have this property. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for many classes of groups, including sofic groups, which include all amenable groups and all residually finite groups. The conjecture is related to the zero-divisor conjecture and to the theory of von Neumann algebras, where direct finiteness is a consequence of the existence of a trace. The conjecture is one of Kaplansky's conjectures on group rings, and it remains open.

History

Kaplansky stated the conjecture in the 1960s, as part of his work on group rings and operator algebras. The conjecture became a central problem in the theory of group rings, and it was studied in connection with the zero-divisor conjecture and the idempotent conjecture. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of groups: for amenable groups, for residually finite groups, and for sofic groups, which are groups that can be approximated by finite symmetric groups. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in the theory of group rings. The conjecture is related to the Gottschalk surjunctivity conjecture, which is the analogous statement for cellular automata.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The direct finiteness conjecture is known for sofic groups, which include all amenable groups, all residually finite groups, and all linear groups. It is open in general. The conjecture is implied by the zero-divisor conjecture, and it is related to the theory of von Neumann algebras, where the group von Neumann algebra of any group is directly finite because it has a faithful trace. The conjecture is one of Kaplansky's conjectures, and it is widely believed to be true. No group ring is known to fail direct finiteness, and the conjecture is a test case for the existence of non-sofic groups.

Math Proof

A ring R is directly finite if ab = 1 implies ba = 1 for all a, b in R. Finite matrices over a field are directly finite, because a one-sided inverse of a matrix is a two-sided inverse by a dimension argument. Group rings are infinite-dimensional, so the dimension argument does not apply, and the conjecture is that the group structure prevents a one-sided inverse from existing without a two-sided inverse. For sofic groups, the conjecture is proved by approximating the group by finite symmetric groups, where direct finiteness holds, and passing to the limit. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the group ring has no non-trivial idempotents of a certain form, and it is related to the zero-divisor conjecture. Formally: for every field K and every group G, the group ring K[G] is directly finite.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If K is a field and G is a group, and a, b in K[G] satisfy ab = 1, does ba = 1 follow? Equivalently, is every group ring directly finite? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Kaplansky Direct Finiteness Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_directfin():
    # In M_2(Q), a one-sided inverse is two-sided: verify on explicit invertible matrices.
    import random
    random.seed(12345)
    lines = ["Direct finiteness check: 2x2 rational matrices, one-sided inverses are two-sided."]
    ok = 0
    for _ in range(5):
        while True:
            A = [[random.randint(-3, 3) for _ in range(2)] for _ in range(2)]
            det = A[0][0] * A[1][1] - A[0][1] * A[1][0]
            if det != 0:
                break
        B = [[A[1][1] / det, -A[0][1] / det], [-A[1][0] / det, A[0][0] / det]]
        def mm(X, Y):
            return [[X[0][0] * Y[0][0] + X[0][1] * Y[1][0], X[0][0] * Y[0][1] + X[0][1] * Y[1][1]],
                    [X[1][0] * Y[0][0] + X[1][1] * Y[1][0], X[1][0] * Y[0][1] + X[1][1] * Y[1][1]]]
        ab = mm(A, B)
        ba = mm(B, A)
        good = all(abs(ab[i][j] - (1 if i == j else 0)) < 1e-9 for i in range(2) for j in range(2)) and \
            all(abs(ba[i][j] - (1 if i == j else 0)) < 1e-9 for i in range(2) for j in range(2))
        ok += good
    lines.append("Random invertible matrices tested: 5, with AB = BA = I in all cases: %s" % (ok == 5))
    lines.append("Range limit: finite matrices over Q; direct finiteness is open for group rings.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_directfin()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Kaplansky Direct Finiteness Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that one-sided inverses of random 2x2 rational matrices are two-sided, which does not establish direct finiteness for group rings")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_directfin():
    # In M_2(Q), a one-sided inverse is two-sided: verify on explicit invertible matrices.
    import random
    random.seed(12345)
    lines = ["Direct finiteness check: 2x2 rational matrices, one-sided inverses are two-sided."]
    ok = 0
    for _ in range(5):
        while True:
            A = [[random.randint(-3, 3) for _ in range(2)] for _ in range(2)]
            det = A[0][0] * A[1][1] - A[0][1] * A[1][0]
            if det != 0:
                break
        B = [[A[1][1] / det, -A[0][1] / det], [-A[1][0] / det, A[0][0] / det]]
        def mm(X, Y):
            return [[X[0][0] * Y[0][0] + X[0][1] * Y[1][0], X[0][0] * Y[0][1] + X[0][1] * Y[1][1]],
                    [X[1][0] * Y[0][0] + X[1][1] * Y[1][0], X[1][0] * Y[0][1] + X[1][1] * Y[1][1]]]
        ab = mm(A, B)
        ba = mm(B, A)
        good = all(abs(ab[i][j] - (1 if i == j else 0)) < 1e-9 for i in range(2) for j in range(2)) and \
            all(abs(ba[i][j] - (1 if i == j else 0)) < 1e-9 for i in range(2) for j in range(2))
        ok += good
    lines.append("Random invertible matrices tested: 5, with AB = BA = I in all cases: %s" % (ok == 5))
    lines.append("Range limit: finite matrices over Q; direct finiteness is open for group rings.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Kaplansky Direct Finiteness Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: If K is a field and G is a group, and a, b in K[G] satisfy ab = 1, does ba = 1 follow? Equivalently, is every group ring directly finite?")
    payload = eng_directfin()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: verifying that one-sided inverses of random 2x2 rational matrices are two-sided, which does not establish direct finiteness for group rings")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Atiyah Conjecture on L2-Betti Numbers JAH-USEQALS-000011 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Atiyah conjecture is a collective term for a number of statements about the possible values of L2-Betti numbers. Michael Atiyah introduced L2-cohomology in 1976 and computed examples in which the L2-Betti numbers were rational, so he asked whether they are always rational. The most general open form asks: if a group G has a bound on the orders of its finite subgroups, are the L2-Betti numbers of a finite CW-complex with fundamental group G rational, with denominators dividing the least common multiple of the orders of the finite subgroups? For torsion-free groups, this says the L2-Betti numbers are integers, which would generalise the zero-divisor conjecture. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for many classes of groups, and counterexamples are known when there is no bound on the finite subgroups.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If G is a group with a bound on the orders of its finite subgroups, are the L2-Betti numbers of any finite CW-complex with fundamental group G rational, with denominators dividing the least common multiple of the subgroup orders? The Atiyah conjecture is a collective term for a number of statements about the possible values of L2-Betti numbers. Michael Atiyah introduced L2-cohomology in 1976 and computed examples in which the L2-Betti numbers were rational, so he asked whether they are always rational. The most general open form asks: if a group G has a bound on the orders of its finite subgroups, are the L2-Betti numbers of a finite CW-complex with fundamental group G rational, with denominators dividing the least common multiple of the orders of the finite subgroups? For torsion-free groups, this says the L2-Betti numbers are integers, which would generalise the zero-divisor conjecture. The conjecture is open in general, although it is known for many classes of groups, and counterexamples are known when there is no bound on the finite subgroups.

History

Atiyah introduced L2-cohomology in 1976 and asked whether the L2-Betti numbers are always rational. The problem was developed by Linnell, Lück, and others, who proved the conjecture for many classes of groups, including free groups, where the L2-Betti numbers are integers. In 2009, Tim Austin showed that without a bound on the orders of the finite subgroups, the L2-Betti numbers can be transcendental, so the boundedness hypothesis is necessary. The conjecture for torsion-free groups, which says the L2-Betti numbers are integers, is still open in general, and it is related to the zero-divisor conjecture and the Kaplansky conjectures. The conjecture is one of the central problems in the theory of L2-invariants.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Atiyah conjecture is known for free groups, for elementary amenable groups, and for many other classes. It is open in general for torsion-free groups. Austin's 2009 result shows that without a bound on the finite subgroups, the L2-Betti numbers can be any non-negative real number, so the boundedness condition is essential. The conjecture for torsion-free groups would imply the zero-divisor conjecture, because a zero divisor in the group ring would give a non-integral L2-Betti number. The conjecture is a central problem in the theory of L2-invariants, and it is related to the Farrell-Jones conjecture and the Baum-Connes conjecture.

The strategy is to take the strongest known method for this family, algebra, and to apply it in the sharpest available form: the computation in the demo checks the claim on a finite range, the formal statement is written out in full, and the obstacles are identified explicitly. The attempt is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED throughout, and it must be read as a design study, not as a verified solution.

Math Proof

Let G be a discrete group and let X be a finite CW-complex with fundamental group G. The L2-Betti numbers b_p^(2)(X) are the von Neumann dimensions of the L2-cohomology groups of the universal cover of X. They are non-negative real numbers, and for a finite complex they are finite. The Atiyah conjecture says that if the orders of the finite subgroups of G are bounded by some integer, then the L2-Betti numbers are rational with denominators dividing the least common multiple of those orders. For torsion-free G, this says the L2-Betti numbers are integers. The conjecture is proved for free groups by Linnell, using the fact that the group von Neumann algebra of a free group is a factor with a trace. Formally: under the bounded-torsion hypothesis, the L2-Betti numbers are rational with bounded denominators.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If G is a group with a bound on the orders of its finite subgroups, are the L2-Betti numbers of any finite CW-complex with fundamental group G rational, with denominators dividing the least common multiple of the subgroup orders? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Atiyah Conjecture on L2-Betti Numbers would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_topo():
    lines = ["Topology check: Euler characteristic and signature from cell counts."]
    lines.append("Torus (1 vertex, 2 edges, 1 face): chi = %d" % (1 - 2 + 1))
    lines.append("Sphere S^2 (tetrahedron: 4 vertices, 6 edges, 4 faces): chi = %d" % (4 - 6 + 4))
    lines.append("Complex projective plane CP^2 (cells in dimensions 0, 2, 4): chi = %d" % 3)
    lines.append("CP^2 intersection form [1]: signature = %d" % 1)
    lines.append("Sphere S^4 (cells in dimensions 0 and 4): chi = %d, signature = %d" % (2, 0))
    lines.append("Range limit: textbook cell counts; the open conjectures concern all manifolds.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_topo()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Atiyah Conjecture on L2-Betti Numbers")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing Euler characteristics and signatures of standard manifolds, which are the classical analogues of L2-Betti numbers, and does not address the conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

    return sieve(n)

        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3

        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_topo():
    lines = ["Topology check: Euler characteristic and signature from cell counts."]
    lines.append("Torus (1 vertex, 2 edges, 1 face): chi = %d" % (1 - 2 + 1))
    lines.append("Sphere S^2 (tetrahedron: 4 vertices, 6 edges, 4 faces): chi = %d" % (4 - 6 + 4))
    lines.append("Complex projective plane CP^2 (cells in dimensions 0, 2, 4): chi = %d" % 3)
    lines.append("CP^2 intersection form [1]: signature = %d" % 1)
    lines.append("Sphere S^4 (cells in dimensions 0 and 4): chi = %d, signature = %d" % (2, 0))
    lines.append("Range limit: textbook cell counts; the open conjectures concern all manifolds.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Atiyah Conjecture on L2-Betti Numbers")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: If G is a group with a bound on the orders of its finite subgroups, are the L2-Betti numbers of any finite CW-complex with fundamental group G rational, with denominators dividing the least common multiple of the subgroup orders?")
    payload = eng_topo()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: computing Euler characteristics and signatures of standard manifolds, which are the classical analogues of L2-Betti numbers, and does not address the conjecture")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Farrell-Jones Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000012 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Farrell-Jones conjecture asserts that the K-theory and L-theory of the group ring of a group G can be computed from the homology of the classifying space of G, via the assembly map. More precisely, it says that the assembly map from the equivariant homology of the classifying space for virtually cyclic subgroups to the K-theory of the group ring is an isomorphism. The conjecture is a central problem in algebraic K-theory and geometric topology, because it would compute the K-theory of group rings, which is otherwise inaccessible, and it would imply the Borel conjecture, the Novikov conjecture, and the Kaplansky idempotent conjecture. The conjecture is known for many classes of groups, including hyperbolic groups and CAT(0) groups, but it remains open in general.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the assembly map from the homology of the classifying space of a group to the K-theory of its group ring an isomorphism? The Farrell-Jones conjecture asserts that the K-theory and L-theory of the group ring of a group G can be computed from the homology of the classifying space of G, via the assembly map. More precisely, it says that the assembly map from the equivariant homology of the classifying space for virtually cyclic subgroups to the K-theory of the group ring is an isomorphism. The conjecture is a central problem in algebraic K-theory and geometric topology, because it would compute the K-theory of group rings, which is otherwise inaccessible, and it would imply the Borel conjecture, the Novikov conjecture, and the Kaplansky idempotent conjecture. The conjecture is known for many classes of groups, including hyperbolic groups and CAT(0) groups, but it remains open in general.

History

F. Thomas Farrell and Lowell Jones stated the conjecture in 1993, as a generalisation of the Borel conjecture and the Novikov conjecture. The conjecture became a central problem in geometric topology, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved for hyperbolic groups by Bartels, Lück and Reich, for CAT(0) groups by Bartels and Lück, and for many other classes. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in algebraic K-theory. The conjecture is related to the Baum-Connes conjecture, which is the analogous statement for the K-theory of the reduced group C*-algebra, and to the isomorphism conjectures in general.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Farrell-Jones conjecture is known for hyperbolic groups, CAT(0) groups, virtually solvable groups, and many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture implies the Borel conjecture, the Novikov conjecture, and the Kaplansky idempotent conjecture, so it is the strongest of the isomorphism conjectures. The conjecture is also known to imply the vanishing of the Whitehead group for torsion-free groups satisfying it, which would settle the s-cobordism theorem for those groups. The conjecture is a central problem in geometric topology, and it is one of the most actively studied conjectures in the area.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt on Farrell-Jones Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. The strategy is to take the strongest known method for this family, algebra, and to apply it in the sharpest available form: the computation in the demo checks the claim on a finite range, the formal statement is written out in full, and the obstacles are identified explicitly. The attempt does not claim to overcome the obstacle. It proposes a route, namely to combine the best known partial result with a refined estimate, and it records exactly where the route stops: at the same barrier that blocks every known approach. The attempt is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED throughout, and it must be read as a design study, not as a verified solution.

The attempt proposes a route built on the strongest known partial result, and it records exactly where the route stops. Nothing in this section is a verified solution, and the record is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED.

Math Proof

Let G be a group and R a ring. The Farrell-Jones conjecture says that the assembly map H_n^G(E_{VCyc} G; K_R) -> K_n(R[G]) is an isomorphism, where E_{VCyc} G is the classifying space for the family of virtually cyclic subgroups and K_R is the K-theory spectrum. The left-hand side is a homology theory that can be computed from the group, and the right-hand side is the K-theory of the group ring, which is hard to compute directly. The conjecture is a statement about the isomorphism conjectures, which say that the K-theory of a group ring is determined by the group. The Borel conjecture and the Novikov conjecture are consequences of the Farrell-Jones conjecture. Formally: the assembly map is an isomorphism for every group G and every ring R.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is the assembly map from the homology of the classifying space of a group to the K-theory of its group ring an isomorphism? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Farrell-Jones Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_farrell_jones():
    # K0(Z) = Z: 2x2 integer idempotents with small entries have trace 0, 1 or 2.
    def mm(A, B):
        return [[A[0][0] * B[0][0] + A[0][1] * B[1][0], A[0][0] * B[0][1] + A[0][1] * B[1][1]],
                [A[1][0] * B[0][0] + A[1][1] * B[1][0], A[1][0] * B[0][1] + A[1][1] * B[1][1]]]
    found = []
    for a in range(-2, 3):
        for b in range(-2, 3):
            for c in range(-2, 3):
                for d in range(-2, 3):
                    E = [[a, b], [c, d]]
                    if mm(E, E) == E:
                        found.append(a + d)
    return "\n".join([
        "Farrell-Jones check: idempotents in M_2(Z) with entries in [-2, 2].",
        "Idempotents found: %d" % len(found),
        "Traces seen: %s (K0(Z) = Z is generated by the rank)" % str(sorted(set(found))),
        "Range limit: small matrices; Farrell-Jones is about K-theory of group rings in general.",
    ])

payload = eng_farrell_jones()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Farrell-Jones Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: enumerating idempotents in 2x2 integer matrices, which are the elements detected by K0, and does not establish the assembly isomorphism")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Signature Attempt — Borel Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000013 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Borel conjecture asserts that a closed aspherical manifold is determined up to homeomorphism by its fundamental group. An aspherical manifold is one whose universal cover is contractible, so all of its homotopy is in the fundamental group; examples are tori and hyperbolic manifolds. The conjecture says that if two closed aspherical manifolds have isomorphic fundamental groups, then they are homeomorphic, and moreover every homotopy equivalence between them is homotopic to a homeomorphism. The conjecture was stated by Armand Borel in the 1950s, and it is one of the central problems in geometric topology. It is known for many classes of manifolds, including non-positively curved manifolds, but it remains open in general.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: If M and N are closed aspherical manifolds with isomorphic fundamental groups, are M and N homeomorphic? Is every homotopy equivalence between them homotopic to a homeomorphism? The Borel conjecture asserts that a closed aspherical manifold is determined up to homeomorphism by its fundamental group. An aspherical manifold is one whose universal cover is contractible, so all of its homotopy is in the fundamental group; examples are tori and hyperbolic manifolds. The conjecture says that if two closed aspherical manifolds have isomorphic fundamental groups, then they are homeomorphic, and moreover every homotopy equivalence between them is homotopic to a homeomorphism. The conjecture was stated by Armand Borel in the 1950s, and it is one of the central problems in geometric topology. It is known for many classes of manifolds, including non-positively curved manifolds, but it remains open in general.

History

Borel stated the conjecture in the 1950s, in his work on the topology of aspherical manifolds. The conjecture became a central problem in geometric topology, and it was studied by Farrell, Jones, and others. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of manifolds: for non-positively curved manifolds by Farrell and Jones, for hyperbolic manifolds, and for manifolds with certain geometric structures. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in geometric topology. The conjecture is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture, and it is related to the Novikov conjecture, which is the analogous statement for the signature of a manifold.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Borel conjecture is known for non-positively curved manifolds, for hyperbolic manifolds, and for many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture, so the known cases of Farrell-Jones give known cases of Borel. The conjecture is also known for manifolds of dimension at most 3, where it follows from the geometrisation theorem. The conjecture is a statement about the rigidity of aspherical manifolds, and it is one of the central open problems in geometric topology. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt on Borel Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. Math Proof

Let M be a closed aspherical manifold, so its universal cover is contractible and its homotopy type is determined by its fundamental group pi_1(M). The Borel conjecture says that the homeomorphism type of M is determined by pi_1(M), and that the structure set of M, which classifies manifolds homotopy equivalent to M, is a single point. The conjecture is a rigidity statement: the fundamental group determines the manifold. The Farrell-Jones conjecture implies Borel because it computes the surgery obstruction groups from the group, and the surgery exact sequence then shows the structure set is trivial. The torus is the basic example: a closed manifold homotopy equivalent to a torus is homeomorphic to a torus, by the work of Farrell and Jones. Formally: for closed aspherical M and N, an isomorphism pi_1(M) -> pi_1(N) is induced by a homeomorphism.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: If M and N are closed aspherical manifolds with isomorphic fundamental groups, are M and N homeomorphic? Is every homotopy equivalence between them homotopic to a homeomorphism? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Borel Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

History

Borel stated the conjecture in the 1950s, in his work on the topology of aspherical manifolds. The conjecture became a central problem in geometric topology, and it was studied by Farrell, Jones, and others. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of manifolds: for non-positively curved manifolds by Farrell and Jones, for hyperbolic manifolds, and for manifolds with certain geometric structures. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in geometric topology. The conjecture is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture, and it is related to the Novikov conjecture, which is the analogous statement for the signature of a manifold.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: If M and N are closed aspherical manifolds with isomorphic fundamental groups, are M and N homeomorphic? Is every homotopy equivalence between them homotopic to a homeomorphism? The Borel conjecture asserts that a closed aspherical manifold is determined up to homeomorphism by its fundamental group. An aspherical manifold is one whose universal cover is contractible, so all of its homotopy is in the fundamental group; examples are tori and hyperbolic manifolds. The conjecture says that if two closed aspherical manifolds have isomorphic fundamental groups, then they are homeomorphic, and moreover every homotopy equivalence between them is homotopic to a homeomorphism. The conjecture was stated by Armand Borel in the 1950s, and it is one of the central problems in geometric topology. It is known for many classes of manifolds, including non-positively curved manifolds, but it remains open in general.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_elliptic(a=0, b=1)
print("Unsolved equation demo: Borel Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 + 1 over small finite fields, the curve being a torus topologically, which does not establish Borel rigidity")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Math Proof

Let M be a closed oriented manifold of dimension 4k, and let u be a cohomology class of the classifying space of the fundamental group. The higher signature is the number , where L(M) is the Hirzebruch L-class and f is the classifying map. The Novikov conjecture says this number depends only on the homotopy type of M, not on the smooth structure. The conjecture is equivalent to the rational injectivity of the assembly map in L-theory, which is the map from the homology of the classifying space to the L-theory of the group ring. The Farrell-Jones conjecture implies Novikov because it says the assembly map is an isomorphism. Formally: for every closed oriented manifold M and every cohomology class u, the higher signature is a homotopy invariant.

History

Novikov stated the conjecture in 1965, in his work on the signature of manifolds. The conjecture became a central problem in geometric topology, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of groups: for groups with finite asymptotic dimension, for hyperbolic groups, and for groups that admit a coarse embedding into Hilbert space. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in topology. The conjecture is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture and by the Baum-Connes conjecture, and it is related to the Borel conjecture, which is the stronger statement that the manifold is determined by its fundamental group.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Are the higher signatures of a closed oriented manifold homotopy invariant? Equivalently, is the assembly map in L-theory injective? The Novikov conjecture asserts that the higher signatures of a closed oriented manifold are homotopy invariant. The signature of a 4k-dimensional manifold is the signature of its intersection form, and it is a homotopy invariant by the Hirzebruch signature theorem. The higher signatures are generalisations: for each cohomology class of the fundamental group, there is a number obtained by pairing the L-class of the manifold with the class, and the conjecture says these numbers are invariant under homotopy equivalences. The conjecture was stated by Sergei Novikov in 1965, and it is one of the central problems in geometric topology. It is known for many classes of groups, but it remains open in general.

Method

The Signature attempt on Novikov Conjecture works from the established partial results and tries to push them one step further. The strategy is to take the strongest known method for this family, algebra, and to apply it in the sharpest available form: the computation in the demo checks the claim on a finite range, the formal statement is written out in full, and the obstacles are identified explicitly. The attempt does not claim to overcome the obstacle. It proposes a route, namely to combine the best known partial result with a refined estimate, and it records exactly where the route stops: at the same barrier that blocks every known approach. The attempt is labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED throughout, and it must be read as a design study, not as a verified solution.

Independent verification of the open problem itself would require a new mathematical argument, peer-reviewed and accepted, and no such argument is claimed here. For the Signature attempt, verification would require an independent check of every step of the proposed route, a demonstration that the obstacle is actually overcome, and peer review. None of that has happened, and the attempt is unverified.
