The Novikov conjecture is known for many classes of groups, including hyperbolic groups, groups of finite asymptotic dimension, and groups that coarsely embed into Hilbert space. Skeptical analysis

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_topo():
    lines = ["Topology check: Euler characteristic and signature from cell counts."]
    lines.append("Torus (1 vertex, 2 edges, 1 face): chi = %d" % (1 - 2 + 1))
    lines.append("Sphere S^2 (tetrahedron: 4 vertices, 6 edges, 4 faces): chi = %d" % (4 - 6 + 4))
    lines.append("Complex projective plane CP^2 (cells in dimensions 0, 2, 4): chi = %d" % 3)
    lines.append("CP^2 intersection form [1]: signature = %d" % 1)
    lines.append("Sphere S^4 (cells in dimensions 0 and 4): chi = %d, signature = %d" % (2, 0))
    lines.append("Range limit: textbook cell counts; the open conjectures concern all manifolds.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_topo()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Novikov Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing the signature of the complex projective plane from its intersection form, which does not establish homotopy invariance of higher signatures")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_topo():
    lines = ["Topology check: Euler characteristic and signature from cell counts."]
    lines.append("Torus (1 vertex, 2 edges, 1 face): chi = %d" % (1 - 2 + 1))
    lines.append("Sphere S^2 (tetrahedron: 4 vertices, 6 edges, 4 faces): chi = %d" % (4 - 6 + 4))
    lines.append("Complex projective plane CP^2 (cells in dimensions 0, 2, 4): chi = %d" % 3)
    lines.append("CP^2 intersection form [1]: signature = %d" % 1)
    lines.append("Sphere S^4 (cells in dimensions 0 and 4): chi = %d, signature = %d" % (2, 0))
    lines.append("Range limit: textbook cell counts; the open conjectures concern all manifolds.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Novikov Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: Are the higher signatures of a closed oriented manifold homotopy invariant? Equivalently, is the assembly map in L-theory injective?")
    payload = eng_topo()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: computing the signature of the complex projective plane from its intersection form, which does not establish homotopy invariance of higher signatures")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Baum-Connes Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000015 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Baum-Connes conjecture asserts that the K-theory of the reduced group C*-algebra of a group G can be computed from the equivariant K-homology of the classifying space of G, via the assembly map. The conjecture is the analytic analogue of the Farrell-Jones conjecture, and it is a central problem in noncommutative geometry and operator algebras. It would imply the Novikov conjecture, the Kadison-Kaplansky conjecture, and the Gromov-Lawson-Rosenberg conjecture on positive scalar curvature. The conjecture is known for many classes of groups, including amenable groups and hyperbolic groups, but it remains open in general. Counterexamples are known for the version with coefficients, found by Higson, Lafforgue and Skandalis in 2002, but the original conjecture without coefficients is still open.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the assembly map from the equivariant K-homology of the classifying space of a group to the K-theory of its reduced group C*-algebra an isomorphism? The Baum-Connes conjecture asserts that the K-theory of the reduced group C*-algebra of a group G can be computed from the equivariant K-homology of the classifying space of G, via the assembly map. The conjecture is the analytic analogue of the Farrell-Jones conjecture, and it is a central problem in noncommutative geometry and operator algebras. It would imply the Novikov conjecture, the Kadison-Kaplansky conjecture, and the Gromov-Lawson-Rosenberg conjecture on positive scalar curvature. The conjecture is known for many classes of groups, including amenable groups and hyperbolic groups, but it remains open in general. Counterexamples are known for the version with coefficients, found by Higson, Lafforgue and Skandalis in 2002, but the original conjecture without coefficients is still open.

History

Paul Baum and Alain Connes stated the conjecture in 1982, as part of their work on noncommutative geometry. The conjecture became a central problem in operator algebras, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved for amenable groups, for hyperbolic groups, and for many other classes, using the Dirac-dual Dirac method. In 2002, Higson, Lafforgue and Skandalis found counterexamples to the conjecture with coefficients, using groups with expander-like properties, but the original conjecture without coefficients remains open. The conjecture is now understood as a statement about the K-theory of group C*-algebras, and it is one of the central open problems in noncommutative geometry.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Baum-Connes conjecture is known for amenable groups, hyperbolic groups, and many other classes. It is open in general without coefficients. The version with coefficients is false, by the counterexamples of Higson, Lafforgue and Skandalis. The conjecture implies the Novikov conjecture and the Kadison-Kaplansky conjecture, so it is a strong statement. The Dirac-dual Dirac method is the main tool for proving the conjecture, and it works when the group admits a proper action on a space with a suitable Dirac operator. The conjecture is one of the central open problems in noncommutative geometry, and it is actively studied.

Math Proof

Let G be a locally compact group and C*_r(G) its reduced group C*-algebra. The Baum-Connes conjecture says the assembly map mu: K_*^G(EG) -> K_*(C*_r(G)) is an isomorphism, where EG is the classifying space for proper actions and K_*^G is equivariant K-homology. The left-hand side is a topological invariant of the group, and the right-hand side is the K-theory of the operator algebra, which is hard to compute. The conjecture is the analytic version of the Farrell-Jones conjecture, and it is proved by the Dirac-dual Dirac method when the group has a gamma element. The counterexamples with coefficients use groups for which the assembly map fails to be surjective, because of the failure of exactness in the relevant sequence. Formally: the assembly map is an isomorphism for every group G.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is the assembly map from the equivariant K-homology of the classifying space of a group to the K-theory of its reduced group C*-algebra an isomorphism? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Baum-Connes Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Baum-Connes check: truncated unilateral shift S on C^6.
S*S = I: False
S S* = I - E_11 (rank-one defect): True
Fredholm index of S (dim ker S* - dim ker S): 1
Range limit: a finite truncation; Baum-Connes is about assembly maps for all groups.

What this does NOT prove: checking the Fredholm index of a truncated unilateral shift, which does not establish the assembly isomorphism.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_baum_connes():
    # Unilateral shift S on C^n (truncation): S*S = I, S S* = I - E_11; Fredholm index 1.
    n = 6
    S = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(1, n):
        S[i][i - 1] = 1
    def mm(X, Y):
        return [[sum(X[i][k] * Y[k][j] for k in range(n)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    def tr(X):
        return [[X[j][i] for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    StS = mm(tr(S), S)
    SSt = mm(S, tr(S))
    eye = [[1 if i == j else 0 for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Baum-Connes check: truncated unilateral shift S on C^%d." % n,
        "S*S = I: %s" % (StS == eye),
        "S S* = I - E_11 (rank-one defect): %s" % (SSt == [[(1 if i == j else 0) - (1 if i == j == 0 else 0) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]),
        "Fredholm index of S (dim ker S* - dim ker S): 1",
        "Range limit: a finite truncation; Baum-Connes is about assembly maps for all groups.",
    ])

payload = eng_baum_connes()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Baum-Connes Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: checking the Fredholm index of a truncated unilateral shift, which does not establish the assembly isomorphism")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_baum_connes():
    # Unilateral shift S on C^n (truncation): S*S = I, S S* = I - E_11; Fredholm index 1.
    n = 6
    S = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(1, n):
        S[i][i - 1] = 1
    def mm(X, Y):
        return [[sum(X[i][k] * Y[k][j] for k in range(n)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    def tr(X):
        return [[X[j][i] for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    StS = mm(tr(S), S)
    SSt = mm(S, tr(S))
    eye = [[1 if i == j else 0 for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    return "\n".join([
        "Baum-Connes check: truncated unilateral shift S on C^%d." % n,
        "S*S = I: %s" % (StS == eye),
        "S S* = I - E_11 (rank-one defect): %s" % (SSt == [[(1 if i == j else 0) - (1 if i == j == 0 else 0) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]),
        "Fredholm index of S (dim ker S* - dim ker S): 1",
        "Range limit: a finite truncation; Baum-Connes is about assembly maps for all groups.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Baum-Connes Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: Is the assembly map from the equivariant K-homology of the classifying space of a group to the K-theory of its reduced group C*-algebra an isomorphism?")
    payload = eng_baum_connes()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: checking the Fredholm index of a truncated unilateral shift, which does not establish the assembly isomorphism")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Grothendieck Standard Conjectures JAH-USEQALS-000016 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The standard conjectures, formulated by Alexander Grothendieck in the 1960s, are a package of conjectures about algebraic cycles on smooth projective varieties. They assert that homological equivalence of algebraic cycles is the same as numerical equivalence, that the Lefschetz operator is induced by an algebraic cycle, that the Künneth components of the diagonal are algebraic, and that the Hodge standard conjecture holds, which is a positivity statement about the intersection pairing. The conjectures would imply the Weil conjectures, which were proved by Deligne by other means, and they would give a theory of motives, which is Grothendieck's envisioned universal cohomology theory. The conjectures are open in general, although they are known for curves, surfaces, and abelian varieties.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Do the standard conjectures on algebraic cycles hold: is homological equivalence the same as numerical equivalence, is the Lefschetz operator algebraic, and is the Kunneth decomposition algebraic? The standard conjectures, formulated by Alexander Grothendieck in the 1960s, are a package of conjectures about algebraic cycles on smooth projective varieties. They assert that homological equivalence of algebraic cycles is the same as numerical equivalence, that the Lefschetz operator is induced by an algebraic cycle, that the Künneth components of the diagonal are algebraic, and that the Hodge standard conjecture holds, which is a positivity statement about the intersection pairing. The conjectures would imply the Weil conjectures, which were proved by Deligne by other means, and they would give a theory of motives, which is Grothendieck's envisioned universal cohomology theory. The conjectures are open in general, although they are known for curves, surfaces, and abelian varieties.

History

Grothendieck formulated the standard conjectures in the 1960s, as part of his program to build a theory of motives. The conjectures were motivated by the Weil conjectures, which Grothendieck wanted to prove by a theory of algebraic cycles. Deligne proved the Weil conjectures in 1974 by a different method, using the theory of weights, but the standard conjectures remain open. In the following decades, they were proved for curves, surfaces, abelian varieties, and some other classes, by the work of Grothendieck, Kleiman, Lieberman, and others. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in algebraic geometry. The conjectures are the foundation of the theory of motives, and they would imply many other conjectures.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The standard conjectures are known for curves, surfaces, abelian varieties, and some other classes of varieties. They are open in general. The conjecture that homological equivalence equals numerical equivalence is known as conjecture D, and the Lefschetz standard conjecture is known as conjecture B. The Hodge standard conjecture is known for abelian varieties and for some other cases. The conjectures would imply the existence of a theory of motives and would give a conceptual proof of the Weil conjectures. No counterexample is known, and the conjectures are widely believed to be true.

Math Proof

Let X be a smooth projective variety over a field. An algebraic cycle is a formal linear combination of subvarieties, and two cycles are homologically equivalent if they have the same cohomology class, and numerically equivalent if they have the same intersection numbers with all cycles of complementary dimension. The standard conjecture D says these two equivalences coincide. The Lefschetz standard conjecture says the inverse of the Lefschetz operator, which is cup product with a hyperplane class, is induced by an algebraic cycle. The Künneth standard conjecture says the Künneth components of the diagonal are algebraic. The Hodge standard conjecture is a positivity statement: the intersection pairing on the primitive cohomology is definite of a certain sign. Together, these conjectures would give a semisimple category of motives. Formally: the standard conjectures hold for every smooth projective variety.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Do the standard conjectures on algebraic cycles hold: is homological equivalence the same as numerical equivalence, is the Lefschetz operator algebraic, and is the Kunneth decomposition algebraic? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Grothendieck Standard Conjectures would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_elliptic(a=0, b=-2)
print("Unsolved equation demo: Grothendieck Standard Conjectures")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 - 2 over small finite fields, which does not address algebraic cycles in general")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Grothendieck Standard Conjectures")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: Do the standard conjectures on algebraic cycles hold: is homological equivalence the same as numerical equivalence, is the Lefschetz operator algebraic, and is the Kunneth decomposition algebraic?")
    payload = eng_elliptic(a=0, b=-2)
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 - 2 over small finite fields, which does not address algebraic cycles in general")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Tate Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000017 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Tate conjecture, stated by John Tate in 1963, asserts that the Galois-invariant cohomology classes on a smooth projective variety over a finite field are exactly the classes of algebraic cycles. In other words, the Tate module of the variety, which is the part of the cohomology fixed by the Galois group, is spanned by algebraic cycles. The conjecture is the arithmetic analogue of the Hodge conjecture, and it is one of the central problems in arithmetic geometry. It is known for divisors, for abelian varieties in some cases, and for K3 surfaces in some cases, but it remains open in general. The conjecture would imply the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture in some forms, and it is related to the standard conjectures.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is every cohomology class on a smooth projective variety over a finite field that is fixed by the Galois group represented by an algebraic cycle? The Tate conjecture, stated by John Tate in 1963, asserts that the Galois-invariant cohomology classes on a smooth projective variety over a finite field are exactly the classes of algebraic cycles. In other words, the Tate module of the variety, which is the part of the cohomology fixed by the Galois group, is spanned by algebraic cycles. The conjecture is the arithmetic analogue of the Hodge conjecture, and it is one of the central problems in arithmetic geometry. It is known for divisors, for abelian varieties in some cases, and for K3 surfaces in some cases, but it remains open in general. The conjecture would imply the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture in some forms, and it is related to the standard conjectures.

History

Tate stated the conjecture in 1963, in a paper on algebraic cycles and poles of zeta functions. The conjecture became a central problem in arithmetic geometry, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved for divisors by Tate himself, for abelian varieties by Faltings in some cases, and for K3 surfaces by Nygaard, Ogus, and others. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in arithmetic geometry. The conjecture is related to the Hodge conjecture, which is the analogous statement over the complex numbers, and to the standard conjectures of Grothendieck.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Tate conjecture is known for divisors, for abelian varieties in some cases, and for K3 surfaces in some cases. It is open in general. The conjecture is known for products of curves and for some other classes of varieties. The Tate conjecture for divisors is a theorem of Tate, and it is the only general case known. The conjecture would imply that the L-function of a variety has a pole of the expected order at s = 1, and it is related to the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture. No counterexample is known, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Math Proof

Let X be a smooth projective variety over a finite field F_q, and let l be a prime different from the characteristic. The Tate conjecture says that the cycle class map from the Chow group of codimension i cycles tensored with Q_l to the Galois-invariant part of the l-adic cohomology H^{2i}(X, Q_l(i)) is surjective. The Galois group acts on the cohomology, and the invariant classes are the Tate classes. The conjecture says every Tate class is algebraic, meaning it comes from an algebraic cycle. For divisors, the conjecture is a theorem, proved by Tate using the theory of the Picard variety. For higher codimensions, the conjecture is open. Formally: the cycle class map is surjective onto the Galois-invariant cohomology.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is every cohomology class on a smooth projective variety over a finite field that is fixed by the Galois group represented by an algebraic cycle? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Tate Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1)
print("Unsolved equation demo: Tate Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 + x + 1 over small finite fields, which does not establish the Tate conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

    payload = eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1)

    print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 + x + 1 over small finite fields, which does not establish the Tate conjecture")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Artin Conjecture on L-Functions JAH-USEQALS-000018 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Artin conjecture asserts that the L-function associated with a non-trivial irreducible complex representation of the Galois group of a number field is entire, meaning it has no poles anywhere in the complex plane. Artin L-functions are generalisations of Dirichlet L-functions and of the Riemann zeta function, and they encode the arithmetic of Galois extensions. The conjecture was stated by Emil Artin in 1923, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic number theory. It is known for representations of dimension 1, by class field theory, and for some representations of dimension 2, by the work of Langlands and Tunnell, but it remains open in general. The conjecture would follow from the Langlands program, which predicts that every Artin L-function is the L-function of an automorphic form.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is every Artin L-function associated with a non-trivial irreducible representation of a Galois group entire? The Artin conjecture asserts that the L-function associated with a non-trivial irreducible complex representation of the Galois group of a number field is entire, meaning it has no poles anywhere in the complex plane. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in the Langlands program. The conjecture is related to the question of whether every Artin L-function is automorphic, which is the Langlands conjecture for Artin representations.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Artin conjecture is known for one-dimensional representations, by class field theory, and for some two-dimensional representations, by the work of Langlands, Tunnell, and others. It is open in general. The conjecture would follow from the Langlands program, which predicts that every Artin L-function is the L-function of a cuspidal automorphic representation. The conjecture is also known for representations that are induced from one-dimensional representations of subgroups, by Brauer's induction theorem. The general case is open, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic number theory. No Artin L-function is known to have a pole, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Math Proof

Let K/Q be a finite Galois extension with group G, and let rho be a non-trivial irreducible complex representation of G. The Artin L-function L(s, rho) is defined by an Euler product over the primes, with local factors determined by the action of Frobenius elements. The conjecture is that L(s, rho) extends to an entire function on the whole complex plane. For one-dimensional rho, the L-function is a Hecke L-function, which is known to be entire by class field theory. For higher-dimensional rho, the L-function is defined by the Euler product for Re(s) > 1, and the conjecture is that it has an analytic continuation to an entire function. Brauer's theorem shows L(s, rho) is meromorphic, so the question is whether it has any poles. Formally: for every non-trivial irreducible rho, L(s, rho) is entire.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is every Artin L-function associated with a non-trivial irreducible representation of a Galois group entire? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_artin(n=10000):
    # Dirichlet character mod 5: chi = [0, 1, -1, -1, 1] for n mod 5 = 0,1,2,3,4.
    chi = {0: 0, 1: 1, 2: -1, 3: -1, 4: 1}
    s1 = sum(chi[k % 5] / k for k in range(1, n + 1))
    s2 = sum(chi[k % 5] / k for k in range(1, 2 * n + 1))
    return "\n".join([
        "Artin L-function check: partial sums of L(1, chi) for the mod-5 character.",
        "Partial sum to n=%d: %.8f" % (n, s1),
        "Partial sum to n=%d: %.8f" % (2 * n, s2),
        "Change on doubling the range: %.3e" % abs(s2 - s1),
        "Range limit: partial sums only; holomorphy and non-vanishing at s = 1 are the open claims.",
    ])

payload = eng_artin()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Artin Conjecture on L-Functions")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing partial sums of a Dirichlet L-value at s = 1, which does not establish holomorphy of Artin L-functions")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Signature Attempt — Leopoldt Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000019 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. Leopoldt's conjecture asserts that the p-adic regulator of a number field is non-zero. The regulator is a determinant built from the p-adic logarithms of a basis of the units of the number field, and the conjecture says this determinant does not vanish. In other words, the global units of a number field remain independent when they are mapped into the p-adic units by the p-adic logarithm. The conjecture was stated by Heinrich-Wolfgang Leopoldt in 1962, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic number theory. It is known for abelian extensions of the rationals, by the work of Brumer, and for many other cases, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is related to the theory of p-adic L-functions and to Iwasawa theory.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the p-adic regulator of a number field non-zero? Equivalently, is the rank of the closure of the global units in the local units equal to the rank of the global units? Leopoldt's conjecture asserts that the p-adic regulator of a number field is non-zero. The regulator is a determinant built from the p-adic logarithms of a basis of the units of the number field, and the conjecture says this determinant does not vanish. In other words, the global units of a number field remain independent when they are mapped into the p-adic units by the p-adic logarithm. The conjecture was stated by Heinrich-Wolfgang Leopoldt in 1962, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic number theory. It is known for abelian extensions of the rationals, by the work of Brumer, and for many other cases, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is related to the theory of p-adic L-functions and to Iwasawa theory.

History

Leopoldt stated the conjecture in 1962, in his work on the p-adic theory of units. The conjecture became a central problem in algebraic number theory, and it was studied by Brumer, who proved it for abelian extensions of the rationals in 1967, using the theory of p-adic L-functions. In the following decades, it was proved for many other cases, including fields with a certain Galois group structure. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in Iwasawa theory. The conjecture is related to the Gross-Kuz'min conjecture, which is a generalisation to the minus part of the class group, and to the theory of p-adic regulators.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

Leopoldt's conjecture is known for abelian extensions of the rationals, by Brumer's theorem, and for many other classes of fields. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the p-adic regulator is non-zero, and it implies that the Leopoldt defect, which measures the failure of the conjecture, is zero. The conjecture is known for fields of small degree by computation, and for fields with a certain Galois group structure. The general case is open, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic number theory. No number field is known to violate the conjecture.

Math Proof

Let K be a number field with r_1 real places and r_2 complex places, so the unit rank is r = r_1 + r_2 - 1 by Dirichlet's theorem. Let p be a prime, and let log_p be the p-adic logarithm. The p-adic regulator is the determinant of the matrix (log_p(sigma_i(epsilon_j))) where epsilon_j is a basis of the units and sigma_i are the embeddings of K into an algebraic closure of Q_p. Leopoldt's conjecture says this determinant is non-zero, so the units remain independent p-adically. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the closure of the global units in the product of the local unit groups has rank r. For abelian fields, Brumer proved the conjecture using the p-adic L-function and the theory of cyclotomic units. Formally: for every number field K and every prime p, the p-adic regulator is non-zero.

The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is the p-adic regulator of a number field non-zero? Equivalently, is the rank of the closure of the global units in the local units equal to the rank of the global units? Science Proof

A solution to Leopoldt Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

The underlying problem remains open, and the public mirror record JAH-USEQAL-000019 carries the status STATUS: OPEN.

History

Leopoldt stated the conjecture in 1962, in his work on the p-adic theory of units. The conjecture became a central problem in algebraic number theory, and it was studied by Brumer, who proved it for abelian extensions of the rationals in 1967, using the theory of p-adic L-functions. In the following decades, it was proved for many other cases, including fields with a certain Galois group structure. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in Iwasawa theory. The conjecture is related to the Gross-Kuz'min conjecture, which is a generalisation to the minus part of the class group, and to the theory of p-adic regulators.

Problem

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the p-adic regulator of a number field non-zero? Equivalently, is the rank of the closure of the global units in the local units equal to the rank of the global units? Leopoldt's conjecture asserts that the p-adic regulator of a number field is non-zero. The regulator is a determinant built from the p-adic logarithms of a basis of the units of the number field, and the conjecture says this determinant does not vanish. In other words, the global units of a number field remain independent when they are mapped into the p-adic units by the p-adic logarithm. The conjecture was stated by Heinrich-Wolfgang Leopoldt in 1962, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic number theory. It is known for abelian extensions of the rationals, by the work of Brumer, and for many other cases, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is related to the theory of p-adic L-functions and to Iwasawa theory.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_units(ds=(2, 3, 5, 6, 7, 10, 11, 13)):
    def fund_unit(d):
        # Continued fraction of sqrt(d). If the period length L is even, the
        # fundamental unit is the convergent at index L-1; if L is odd, use 2L-1.
        a0 = math.isqrt(d)
        m, den, a = 0, 1, a0
        period = []
        while True:
            m = den * a - m
            den = (d - m * m) // den
            a = (a0 + m) // den
            period.append(a)
            if a == 2 * a0:
                break
        L = len(period)
        seq = [a0] + period[:-1]  # convergent at this index gives the fundamental unit
        # convergent p/q of the continued fraction seq
        p_prev, p_cur = 1, seq[0]
        q_prev, q_cur = 0, 1
        for ai in seq[1:]:
            p_prev, p_cur = p_cur, ai * p_cur + p_prev
            q_prev, q_cur = q_cur, ai * q_cur + q_prev
        return p_cur, q_cur
    lines = ["Leopoldt check: fundamental units of real quadratic fields Q(sqrt(d))."]
    for d in ds:
        p, q = fund_unit(d)
        lines.append("d=%d: fundamental unit %d + %d*sqrt(%d), norm %d" % (d, p, q, d, p * p - d * q * q))
    lines.append("Range limit: eight small discriminants; Leopoldt concerns p-adic regulators in general.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_units()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Leopoldt Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing fundamental units of real quadratic fields by continued fractions, which does not establish the non-vanishing of p-adic regulators")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False

            start = i * i

    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):

        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):

    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p

    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

    d = 1

                s += n // d

    return s

    x = n

        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p

    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""

Math Proof

Let p be an odd prime, let zeta_p be a primitive p-th root of unity, and let K+ = Q(zeta_p + zeta_p^{-1}) be the maximal real subfield of the cyclotomic field. The class number h(K+) measures the failure of unique factorisation in the ring of integers of K+. Vandiver's conjecture says p does not divide h(K+). The conjecture is related to the Bernoulli numbers: a prime p is irregular if it divides the numerator of some Bernoulli number B_{2k} with 2k < p - 1, and the irregular primes are the ones where the class number of the full cyclotomic field is divisible by p. The plus part of the class group is the part fixed by complex conjugation, and the conjecture says p does not divide its order. Formally: for every prime p, p does not divide the class number of Q(zeta_p + zeta_p^{-1}).

The conjecture is open in general, and it is considered one of the central problems in the theory of cyclotomic fields.

Method

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_bernoulli(n=46, plimit=47):
    from fractions import Fraction
    B = [Fraction(0)] * (n + 1)
    B[0] = Fraction(1)
    for m in range(1, n + 1):
        s = Fraction(0)
        for k in range(m):
            s += math.comb(m + 1, k) * B[k]
        B[m] = -s / (m + 1)
    lines = ["Bernoulli number check: B_2 .. B_%d computed exactly as fractions." % n]
    for m in range(2, n + 1, 2):
        lines.append("B_%d = %s" % (m, str(B[m])))
    irr = []
    for p in primes_upto(plimit):
        if p < 5:
            continue
        bad = any(B[2 * k].numerator % p == 0 for k in range(1, min((p - 1) // 2, n // 2) + 1))
        if bad:
            irr.append(p)
    lines.append("Irregular primes below %d: %s" % (plimit, str(irr)))
    lines.append("Range limit: Bernoulli numbers to B_%d; Vandiver concerns all irregular primes." % n)
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_bernoulli()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Vandiver Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing Bernoulli numbers and listing irregular primes below 47, which does not establish Vandiver's conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Signature Attempt — Stark Conjectures JAH-USEQALS-000021 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Stark conjectures, formulated by Harold Stark in the 1970s, predict that the leading terms of Artin L-functions at s = 0 are given by the regulators of units in number fields. In the simplest form, for a character chi of the Galois group of an abelian extension, the value L'(0, chi) is related to the logarithm of a unit in the field, and the precise formula involves the regulator of the units. The conjectures are a vast generalisation of the analytic class number formula, which relates the residue of the zeta function at s = 1 to the class number and the regulator. The Stark conjectures are known in some cases, including the rank-one abelian case proved by Stark himself, but they remain open in general. They are central to the theory of L-functions and to the study of units in number fields.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Do the leading terms of Artin L-functions at s = 0 give the regulators of units, as predicted by Stark: is L'(0, chi) related to the logarithm of a unit in the expected way? The Stark conjectures, formulated by Harold Stark in the 1970s, predict that the leading terms of Artin L-functions at s = 0 are given by the regulators of units in number fields. In the simplest form, for a character chi of the Galois group of an abelian extension, the value L'(0, chi) is related to the logarithm of a unit in the field, and the precise formula involves the regulator of the units. The conjectures are a vast generalisation of the analytic class number formula, which relates the residue of the zeta function at s = 1 to the class number and the regulator. The Stark conjectures are known in some cases, including the rank-one abelian case proved by Stark himself, but they remain open in general. They are central to the theory of L-functions and to the study of units in number fields.

History

Stark formulated the conjectures in the 1970s, in his work on L-functions at s = 0. The conjectures became a central problem in algebraic number theory, and they were studied by Tate, who reformulated them in terms of the leading term of the L-function, and by many others. In the following decades, the rank-one abelian case was proved by Stark, and the general abelian case was proved by Tate and others in some forms. The non-abelian case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in the theory of L-functions. The conjectures are related to the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture, which is the analogous statement for elliptic curves, and to the theory of motives.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Stark conjectures are known for the rank-one abelian case, by Stark's theorem, and for some other cases. They are open in general. The conjectures predict the leading term of Artin L-functions at s = 0 in terms of units, and they are the L-function analogue of the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture. The Rubin-Stark conjecture is a refinement that predicts the existence of special units, called Stark units, and it is also open. The conjectures are central to the theory of L-functions, and they are one of the most actively studied problems in algebraic number theory.

Math Proof

Let chi be a character of the Galois group of a finite Galois extension K/k, and let L(s, chi) be the Artin L-function. The Stark conjecture says that the leading term of L(s, chi) at s = 0 is given by a regulator: L(s, chi) ~ (something) x R(chi) x s^r, where r is the order of vanishing and R(chi) is the regulator of a certain group of units. For the rank-one case, the conjecture says L'(0, chi) = - (1/w) sum_{sigma} chi(sigma) log|epsilon^sigma|, where epsilon is a unit in the field and w is the number of roots of unity. The conjecture is a generalisation of the analytic class number formula, which is the case of the trivial character. Formally: the leading term of L(s, chi) at s = 0 is given by the Stark regulator.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Do the leading terms of Artin L-functions at s = 0 give the regulators of units, as predicted by Stark: is L'(0, chi) related to the logarithm of a unit in the expected way? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Stark Conjectures would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

import math

    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_stark():
    # L(0, chi) for the mod-5 character via the exact formula L(0,chi) = -1/5 * sum a chi(a).
    chi = {1: 1, 2: -1, 3: -1, 4: 1}
    val = -sum(a * chi[a] for a in range(1, 5)) / 5.0
    # Partial sum check of the Dirichlet series at s = 0 is divergent; instead verify
    # the class-number formula side: h(Q(sqrt(5))) = 1 and regulator log((1+sqrt5)/2).
    reg = math.log((1 + math.sqrt(5)) / 2)
    return "\n".join([
        "Stark check: L(0, chi) for the mod-5 character from its exact Bernoulli formula.",
        "L(0, chi) = %.6f" % val,
        "Regulator of Q(sqrt(5)): log((1+sqrt(5))/2) = %.6f" % reg,
        "Class number of Q(sqrt(5)): 1",
        "Range limit: one character; Stark conjectures give L'(0, chi) for all characters.",
    ])

payload = eng_stark()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Stark Conjectures")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: computing L(0, chi) for the mod-5 character from its exact formula, which does not establish the Stark conjectures")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_stark():
    # L(0, chi) for the mod-5 character via the exact formula L(0,chi) = -1/5 * sum a chi(a).
    chi = {1: 1, 2: -1, 3: -1, 4: 1}
    val = -sum(a * chi[a] for a in range(1, 5)) / 5.0
    # Partial sum check of the Dirichlet series at s = 0 is divergent; instead verify
    # the class-number formula side: h(Q(sqrt(5))) = 1 and regulator log((1+sqrt5)/2).
    reg = math.log((1 + math.sqrt(5)) / 2)
    return "\n".join([
        "Stark check: L(0, chi) for the mod-5 character from its exact Bernoulli formula.",
        "L(0, chi) = %.6f" % val,
        "Regulator of Q(sqrt(5)): log((1+sqrt(5))/2) = %.6f" % reg,
        "Class number of Q(sqrt(5)): 1",
        "Range limit: one character; Stark conjectures give L'(0, chi) for all characters.",
    ])

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Stark Conjectures")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: Do the leading terms of Artin L-functions at s = 0 give the regulators of units, as predicted by Stark: is L'(0, chi) related to the logarithm of a unit in the expected way?")
    payload = eng_stark()
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: computing L(0, chi) for the mod-5 character from its exact formula, which does not establish the Stark conjectures")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
