Signature Attempt — Mumford-Tate Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000022 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Mumford-Tate conjecture asserts that the Mumford-Tate group of an abelian variety, which is the algebraic group that encodes the Hodge structure, is equal to the image of the Galois group in the l-adic representation, up to finite index. In other words, the Hodge structure of an abelian variety determines the Galois representation, and the Galois representation is as large as the Hodge structure allows. The conjecture was stated by Mumford and Tate in the 1960s, and it is one of the central problems in arithmetic geometry. It is known for many classes of abelian varieties, including elliptic curves and abelian varieties of low dimension, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is related to the Tate conjecture and to the Hodge conjecture, and it is a test case for the interaction between Hodge theory and Galois representations.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the Mumford-Tate group of an abelian variety equal to the image of the Galois group in the l-adic representation, up to finite index? Equivalently, does the Hodge structure determine the Galois representation? The Mumford-Tate conjecture asserts that the Mumford-Tate group of an abelian variety, which is the algebraic group that encodes the Hodge structure, is equal to the image of the Galois group in the l-adic representation, up to finite index. In other words, the Hodge structure of an abelian variety determines the Galois representation, and the Galois representation is as large as the Hodge structure allows. The conjecture was stated by Mumford and Tate in the 1960s, and it is one of the central problems in arithmetic geometry. It is known for many classes of abelian varieties, including elliptic curves and abelian varieties of low dimension, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is related to the Tate conjecture and to the Hodge conjecture, and it is a test case for the interaction between Hodge theory and Galois representations.

History

Mumford and Tate formulated the conjecture in the 1960s, in their work on the Hodge theory of abelian varieties. The conjecture became a central problem in arithmetic geometry, and it was studied by Deligne, Serre, and others. In the following decades, it was proved for elliptic curves by Serre, for abelian varieties of dimension at most 3, and for many other classes. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in the theory of abelian varieties. The conjecture is related to the Sato-Tate conjecture, which describes the distribution of the Frobenius traces, and which was proved for elliptic curves by Clozel, Harris, Shepherd-Barron and Taylor.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Mumford-Tate conjecture is known for elliptic curves, for abelian varieties of dimension at most 3, and for many other classes. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the image of the Galois representation is open in the Mumford-Tate group, and it implies the Tate conjecture for the variety. The Sato-Tate conjecture, which is a consequence of the Mumford-Tate conjecture for elliptic curves, was proved by Clozel, Harris, Shepherd-Barron and Taylor. The general case is open, and it is one of the central problems in arithmetic geometry.

Math Proof

Let A be an abelian variety over a number field, and let H be its first homology group with its Hodge structure. The Mumford-Tate group MT(A) is the smallest algebraic subgroup of GL(H) that contains the image of the Hodge cocharacter, and it encodes the Hodge structure. The l-adic representation rho_l: Gal -> GL(H tensor Q_l) has image whose Zariski closure is the l-adic monodromy group. The Mumford-Tate conjecture says the connected component of the l-adic monodromy group equals MT(A) tensor Q_l. For an elliptic curve without complex multiplication, MT(A) = GL_2, and the conjecture says the Galois image is open in GL_2(Z_l), which is Serre's open image theorem. Formally: the l-adic monodromy group is the Mumford-Tate group, up to finite index.

Equivalently, does the Hodge structure determine the Galois representation? Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_elliptic(a=2, b=1)
print("Unsolved equation demo: Mumford-Tate Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 + 2x + 1 and checking Sato-Tate statistics, which does not establish the Mumford-Tate conjecture")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Mumford-Tate Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: Is the Mumford-Tate group of an abelian variety equal to the image of the Galois group in the l-adic representation, up to finite index? Equivalently, does the Hodge structure determine the Galois representation?")
    payload = eng_elliptic(a=2, b=1)
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 + 2x + 1 and checking Sato-Tate statistics, which does not establish the Mumford-Tate conjecture")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Fontaine-Mazur Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000023 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Fontaine-Mazur conjecture asserts that every irreducible geometric p-adic Galois representation of the absolute Galois group of a number field comes from algebraic geometry, meaning it occurs in the cohomology of an algebraic variety. A geometric representation is one that is unramified outside finitely many places and de Rham at the places above p, which are the conditions satisfied by the representations coming from geometry. The conjecture was stated by Jean-Marc Fontaine and Barry Mazur in 1995, and it is one of the central problems in the Langlands program. It is known in many cases, including the two-dimensional case over the rationals, by the work of Kisin and Emerton, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is the p-adic analogue of the statement that every Artin representation is modular.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is every irreducible geometric p-adic Galois representation of the absolute Galois group of a number field modular, coming from algebraic geometry? The Fontaine-Mazur conjecture asserts that every irreducible geometric p-adic Galois representation of the absolute Galois group of a number field comes from algebraic geometry, meaning it occurs in the cohomology of an algebraic variety. A geometric representation is one that is unramified outside finitely many places and de Rham at the places above p, which are the conditions satisfied by the representations coming from geometry. The conjecture was stated by Jean-Marc Fontaine and Barry Mazur in 1995, and it is one of the central problems in the Langlands program. It is known in many cases, including the two-dimensional case over the rationals, by the work of Kisin and Emerton, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is the p-adic analogue of the statement that every Artin representation is modular.

History

Fontaine and Mazur stated the conjecture in 1995, in their work on p-adic Galois representations. The conjecture became a central problem in the Langlands program, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved in many cases: for two-dimensional representations over the rationals by Kisin and Emerton, using the theory of p-adic modular forms, and for many other cases by the work of Scholze and others. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in arithmetic geometry. The conjecture is related to the question of whether every geometric Galois representation is automorphic, which is the Fontaine-Mazur-Langlands conjecture.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Fontaine-Mazur conjecture is known for two-dimensional representations over the rationals, and for many other cases. It is open in general. The conjecture implies that every geometric p-adic Galois representation is modular, and it is a central problem in the Langlands program. The conjecture is known for representations that are potentially Barsotti-Tate, and for many other classes. The general case is open, and it is one of the most actively studied conjectures in arithmetic geometry. No geometric representation is known that does not come from geometry, and the conjecture is widely believed to be true.

Math Proof

Let K be a number field and let rho: Gal(K-bar/K) -> GL_n(Q_p-bar) be a continuous representation. The representation is geometric if it is unramified outside a finite set of places and de Rham at the places above p, which means the associated filtered phi-module has the expected structure. The Fontaine-Mazur conjecture says that every irreducible geometric representation occurs in the etale cohomology of a smooth projective variety over K, or equivalently that it is modular, meaning it comes from an automorphic form. For n = 2 and K = Q, the conjecture is a theorem of Kisin and Emerton, proved using the p-adic Langlands correspondence. The general case is open. Formally: every irreducible geometric p-adic Galois representation is modular.

Science Proof

The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_elliptic(a=-1, b=2)
print("Unsolved equation demo: Fontaine-Mazur Conjecture")
print(payload)

    return tot

def eng_elliptic(a=1, b=1, ps=(5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23, 29, 31)):
    lines = ["Elliptic curve check: points on y^2 = x^3 + %d x + %d over F_p." % (a, b)]
    traces = []
    for p in ps:
        cnt = 1  # point at infinity
        for x in range(p):
            rhs = (x ** 3 + a * x + b) % p
            sols = sum(1 for y in range(p) if (y * y) % p == rhs)
            cnt += sols
        ap = p + 1 - cnt
        traces.append(ap)
        lines.append("p=%d: points = %d, trace a_p = %d, |a_p| <= 2 sqrt(p): %s"
                     % (p, cnt, ap, abs(ap) <= 2 * math.sqrt(p)))
    lines.append("Normalised traces a_p/(2 sqrt(p)): %s" % str(["%.3f" % (t / (2 * math.sqrt(p))) for t, p in zip(traces, ps)]))
    lines.append("Range limit: nine small primes; the Sato-Tate distribution is a statement about all p.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

def main():
    print("Unsolved equation program: Fontaine-Mazur Conjecture")
    print("Edition: signature")
    print("STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED")
    print("Statement: Is every irreducible geometric p-adic Galois representation of the absolute Galois group of a number field modular, coming from algebraic geometry?")
    payload = eng_elliptic(a=-1, b=2)
    print(payload)
    print("What this does NOT prove: counting points on the elliptic curve y^2 = x^3 - x + 2 over small finite fields, which does not address geometric Galois representations")
    print("Attempt summary: a design-study route is proposed and is UNVERIFIED; no verified solution is claimed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Signature Attempt — Bass Trace Conjecture JAH-USEQALS-000024 · signature

Evidence: design-study

This Signature record presents an attempted approach to {t}, an open problem in {f}. The problem asks: {eq} The attempt is a design study, labelled STATUS: ATTEMPT — NOT VERIFIED, and it does not claim a verified solution. The Bass trace conjecture, also called the Hattori-Stallings rank conjecture, asserts that the trace of an idempotent in the group ring of a group is supported on the conjugacy classes of elements of finite order. In other words, if e is an idempotent in the group ring Z[G] or C[G], then its Hattori-Stallings trace, which is a class function on the group, vanishes on every element of infinite order. The conjecture was stated by Hyman Bass in the 1970s, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic K-theory. It is known for many classes of groups, including amenable groups and hyperbolic groups, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture, and it is related to the Kaplansky idempotent conjecture.

Statement

The problem, stated exactly, is: Is the Hattori-Stallings trace of an idempotent in the group ring of a group always an integer linear combination of conjugacy classes of finite-order elements? Equivalently, does the trace vanish on infinite-order classes? The Bass trace conjecture, also called the Hattori-Stallings rank conjecture, asserts that the trace of an idempotent in the group ring of a group is supported on the conjugacy classes of elements of finite order. In other words, if e is an idempotent in the group ring Z[G] or C[G], then its Hattori-Stallings trace, which is a class function on the group, vanishes on every element of infinite order. The conjecture was stated by Hyman Bass in the 1970s, and it is one of the central problems in algebraic K-theory. It is known for many classes of groups, including amenable groups and hyperbolic groups, but it remains open in general. The conjecture is implied by the Farrell-Jones conjecture, and it is related to the Kaplansky idempotent conjecture.

History

Bass stated the conjecture in the 1970s, in his work on the K-theory of group rings. The conjecture became a central problem in algebraic K-theory, and it was studied by many authors. In the following decades, it was proved for many classes of groups: for amenable groups, for hyperbolic groups, and for groups satisfying the Farrell-Jones conjecture. The general case remains open, and it is considered one of the central problems in the K-theory of group rings. The conjecture is related to the question of whether the Hattori-Stallings trace detects the rank of a projective module, which is the original motivation: the trace of the identity is the rank, and the conjecture says the trace is concentrated on finite-order elements.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case is far wilder than the small cases that are understood. The classification works in low dimensions and for special classes, but the general object has pathologies that the available structure theorems do not control. Homological methods give necessary conditions but not sufficient ones, and computation on small examples cannot reach the general case. The attempt in the Signature mirror record is subject to the same limits: it computes a verified partial result on a small example and states plainly what that computation does not establish.

What Is Known

The Bass trace conjecture is known for amenable groups, for hyperbolic groups, and for groups satisfying the Farrell-Jones conjecture. It is open in general. The conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the Hattori-Stallings trace of a finitely generated projective module over the group ring is supported on finite-order conjugacy classes. The conjecture implies that the rank of a projective module is an integer, which is the Hattori-Stallings rank conjecture. The conjecture is a central problem in algebraic K-theory, and it is one of the most actively studied conjectures in the area.

Math Proof

Let G be a group and let P be a finitely generated projective module over the group ring R[G]. The Hattori-Stallings trace of P is a class function on G, defined by the trace of the idempotent that presents P. The Bass conjecture says this trace is supported on the conjugacy classes of elements of finite order, so it vanishes on every element of infinite order. For the free module R[G] itself, the trace is 1 at the identity and 0 elsewhere, which is consistent with the conjecture. The conjecture is proved for groups satisfying the Farrell-Jones conjecture, because the assembly map computes the K-theory and the trace can be read off from the homology. Formally: for every finitely generated projective module P over Z[G], the Hattori-Stallings trace of P vanishes on infinite-order elements.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the statement be written in a formal language with quantifiers over the natural numbers and the complex numbers as appropriate. The claim is a universally quantified statement: for all objects of the stated kind, the stated equation or inequality holds. In symbols, the problem is: Is the Hattori-Stallings trace of an idempotent in the group ring of a group always an integer linear combination of conjugacy classes of finite-order elements? Equivalently, does the trace vanish on infinite-order classes? The formal content is the universal closure of this statement over the domain in question, with the primitive notions being those of arithmetic and analysis: addition, multiplication, primality, and the functions named in the statement. A formal derivation of the claim would be a finite sequence of steps from the axioms of arithmetic, and no such derivation is known. The partial results quoted in this record are formal statements about finite ranges, and they are proved by finite verification, which is a different and much weaker kind of statement.

Science Proof

A solution to Bass Trace Conjecture would reshape algebra and its neighbours. The problem concerns the basic structures of algebra, rings, modules, group rings and varieties, and its answer would determine whether the classification tools of algebra work as expected. The methods developed for it feed into algebraic geometry, number theory, topology and mathematical physics, where the same structures appear as symmetries and as invariants. The problem matters because it is a precise question about whether a natural construction behaves the way it should, and its answer would tell us how much of the algebraic world is under control.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

def sieve(n):
    is_p = [True] * (n + 1)
    is_p[0] = is_p[1] = False
    for i in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1):
        if is_p[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_p[start:n + 1:step] = [False] * ((n - start) // step + 1)
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(is_p) if v]

def primes_upto(n):
    return sieve(n)

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return n == 2
    d = 3
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 2
    return True

def phi(n):
    result = n
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
            result -= result // p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        result -= result // x
    return result

def divisor_sum(n):
    s = 0
    d = 1
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            s += d
            if d != n // d:
                s += n // d
        d += 1
    return s

def rad(n):
    r = 1
    x = n
    p = 2
    while p * p <= x:
        if x % p == 0:
            r *= p
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
        p += 1 if p == 2 else 2
    if x > 1:
        r *= x
    return r

def _eta(s, terms):
    """Dirichlet eta(s) by alternating partial sum with first-order Euler
    tail correction (error ~ N^(-3/2) on the critical line)."""
    import cmath
    tot = complex(0, 0)
    for k in range(1, terms + 1):
        tot += ((-1) ** (k - 1)) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(k))
    tot += 0.5 * ((-1) ** terms) * cmath.exp(-s * cmath.log(terms + 1))
    return tot

def eng_basstrace():
    # Idempotent 2x2 integer matrices E with E^2 = E; Hattori-Stallings trace = trace(E).
    def matmul(A, B):
        return [[A[0][0] * B[0][0] + A[0][1] * B[1][0], A[0][0] * B[0][1] + A[0][1] * B[1][1]],
                [A[1][0] * B[0][0] + A[1][1] * B[1][0], A[1][0] * B[0][1] + A[1][1] * B[1][1]]]
    found = []
    for a in range(-2, 3):
        for b in range(-2, 3):
            for c in range(-2, 3):
                for d in range(-2, 3):
                    E = [[a, b], [c, d]]
                    if matmul(E, E) == E:
                        found.append((E, a + d))
    lines = ["Bass trace check: 2x2 integer idempotent matrices with entries in [-2, 2]."]
    lines.append("Idempotents found: %d" % len(found))
    lines.append("Their traces: %s" % str(sorted(set(t for _, t in found))))
    lines.append("All traces are integers (0, 1 or 2): %s" % all(t in (0, 1, 2) for _, t in found))
    lines.append("Range limit: small matrices only; the Bass conjecture is about all idempotents in group rings.")
    return "\n".join(lines)

payload = eng_basstrace()
print("Unsolved equation demo: Bass Trace Conjecture")
print(payload)
print("What this does NOT prove: enumerating 2x2 integer idempotent matrices and verifying their traces are integers, which does not establish the trace conjecture for group rings")
h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + h1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + h2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if h1 == h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
